Output Perturbation for Differentially Private Convex Optimization: Faster and More General
Andrew Lowy Meisam Razaviyayn

Abstract
Finding efficient, easily implementable differentially private (DP) algorithms that offer strong excess risk bounds is an important problem in modern machine learning. To date, most work has focused on private empirical risk minimization (ERM) or private stochastic convex optimization (SCO), which corresponds to population loss minimization. However, there are often other objectives—such as fairness, adversarial robustness, or sensitivity to outliers—besides average performance that are not captured in the classical ERM/SCO setups. Further, most recent work in private SCO has focused on ( ε , δ ) (\varepsilon,\delta) -differential privacy ( δ > 0 \delta>0 ), whereas proving tight excess risk and runtime bounds for ( ε , 0 ) (\varepsilon,0) -differential privacy remains a challenging open problem. Our first contribution is to provide the tightest known ( ε , 0 ) (\varepsilon,0) -differentially private expected population loss bounds and fastest runtimes for smooth and strongly convex loss functions. In particular, for SCO with well-conditioned smooth and strongly convex loss functions, we provide a linear-time algorithm with optimal excess risk. For our second contribution, we study DP optimization for a broad class of tilted loss functions—which can be used to promote fairness or robustness, and are not necessarily of ERM form. We establish the first known differentially private excess risk and runtime bounds for optimizing this class; under smoothness and strong convexity assumptions, our bounds are near-optimal. For our third contribution, we specialize our theory to DP adversarial training . Our results are achieved using perhaps the simplest yet practical differentially private algorithm: output perturbation. Although this method is not novel conceptually, our novel implementation scheme and analysis show that the power of this method to achieve strong privacy, utility, and runtime guarantees has not been fully appreciated in prior works.



1
Introduction

Answering many science and engineering questions relies on computing solutions to optimization problems (to find the best design parameters). The solution of these optimization problems typically leads to the design of a system or a model for the engineering (or machine learning) task at hand. By observing the parameters of the system/model, an adversary can gain insights to the data used by the engineer/learner. For example, through observing the behavior of a trained machine learning model, an adversary can learn about the private data used during training procedure; or in smart grids, by observing the price of electricity, an adversary can infer power consumption patterns of the individual users. Other examples include eavesdropping the location of cellphone users through the transmission patterns in wireless networks, or revealing individuals’ genomics data by observing the results of data processing procedures on biological data. Finding efficient, easily implementable
differentially private
algorithms that offer strong excess loss/risk bounds is key in all these applications.

Differential privacy
(DP)
[
DMNS06
]
provides a rigorous guarantee that no adversary can infer much about any individual’s data by observing the output of an algorithm. More precisely, a randomized algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to be
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private if for all measurable subsets
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mbox{range}(\mathcal{A})
and all
n
-element data sets
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
which differ by at most one observation (i.e.
|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2
), we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}(X)\in\mathcal{K})\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}(X^{\prime})\in\mathcal{K})e^{\varepsilon}+\delta,

where the probability is (solely) over the randomness of
\mathcal{A}

[
DR14
]
. We refer to data sets differing in only one observation (
|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2
) as
adjacent
. If
\delta=0,
we may say an algorithm is
\varepsilon
-differentially private.
While large values of
\varepsilon
can still provide some privacy,
\delta\ll 1
is necessary for meaningful privacy guarantees. In fact,
\delta\ll\frac{1}{n}
is typically desirable: otherwise, a model may leak the full data of a few individuals and still satisfy the privacy constraint
[
DR14
]
.
To clarify further,
[
DR14
]
explain:

“Even when
\delta>0
is negligible…there are theoretical distinctions between
(\varepsilon,0)
- and
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differential privacy. Chief among these is what amounts to a switch of quantification order.
(\varepsilon,0)
-differential
privacy ensures that, for every run of the mechanism
\mathcal{A}(X)
, the output observed is (almost) equally likely to be observed on every neighboring database, simultaneously. In contrast
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differential privacy
says that for every pair of neighboring databases
X
,
X^{\prime}
, it is extremely
unlikely that,
ex post facto
the observed value
\mathcal{A}(X)
will be much more
or much less likely to be generated when the database is
X
than when
the database is
X^{\prime}
. However, given an output
\xi\sim\mathcal{A}(X)
it may be possible to find a database
X^{\prime}
such that
\xi
is much more likely to be produced on
X^{\prime}
than it is when the database is
X
. That is, the mass of
\xi
in the distribution
A(X^{\prime})
may be substantially larger than its mass in the distribution
\mathcal{A}(x)
.”

Whereas a lot of the literature has focused on efficient algorithms for
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private algorithms, the important case of
\delta=0
has been neglected.
The first contribution
of this work is to fill this void. See
table

3
in
appendix

A
for a summary of these results.
In particular, we provide
the tightest known
(\varepsilon,0)
-differentially private population loss bounds and fastest runtimes
under strong convexity and smoothness assumptions; we achieve
near-optimal excess risk in nearly linear time
: see Corollary
4.2
.

Assume the parameters of a machine learning model are trained via solving the minimization problem:

\tilde{w}(X)\approx\arg\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(w,X).

(1)

In the case of empirical risk minimization (ERM), where
F(w,X)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i}),

algorithms for maintaining differential privacy while solving
eq.

1
are well studied
[
CMS11
,
BST14
,
ZZMW17
,
WYX17
]
. Here (and throughout)
X=(x_{1}\cdots,x_{n})
is a data set with observations in some set
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}.

More recently, several works have also considered private stochastic convex optimization (SCO), where the goal is to minimize the expected population loss
F(w,\mathcal{D})=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}[f(w,x)],
given access to
n
i.i.d. samples
X=(x_{1}\cdots,x_{n})

[
BFTGT19
,
FKT20
,
AMU20
]
. However, the algorithms in these works are only differentially private for
\delta>0,
which, as discussed earlier, provides substantially weaker privacy guarantees.
1
1

1

For certain function classes, the ERM results of
[
BST14
]
and
[
ZZMW17
]
also extend to expected population loss (SCO) bounds for
\delta=0
, but we improve or match the loss bounds and improve the runtime bounds for every function class. See
table

3
for details.
Therefore, providing efficient, practical algorithms for
(\varepsilon,0)
-differentially private SCO is an important gap that we fill in the present work.

Our second main contribution
is differentially private convex optimization for
loss functions that are not of ERM or SCO form
. While ERM and SCO are useful if average performance is the goal, there are situations where another objective besides average performance is desirable. For example, one may want to train a machine learning model that ensures some subsets of the population are treated fairly (see e.g.
[
DTD15
]
), or one that is robust to corrupted data or adversarial attacks
[
GSS15
]
, or one that has lower variance to allow for potentially better generalization. One may also want to diminish the effect of outliers or increase sensitivity to outliers. In these cases, it may be more fruitful to consider an alternative loss function that is not of ERM form. For example, the max-loss function

F(w,X)=\max\{f(w,x_{1}),...f(w,x_{n})\}

provides a model that has good “worst-case” performance and such
F
is clearly not of ERM form. The recently proposed
tilted ERM (TERM)
framework
[
LBSS20
]
aims to address these shortcomings of standard ERM and encompasses the max-loss mentioned above. More generally, a benefit of the TERM framework is that it allows for a continuum of solutions between average and max loss, which, for example, can be calibrated to promote a desired level of fairness in the the machine learning model
[
LBSS20
]
. Another interpretation of TERM is that as
\tau
increases, the variance of the model decreases, while the bias increases. Thus,
\tau
can also be tuned to improve the generalization of the model via the bias/variance tradeoff.

Existing differentially private utility and runtime results have all been derived specifically for standard ERM or SCO and therefore would not apply to TERM objectives. To address this limitation, we provide
the first excess risk and runtime bounds for differentially private TERM
: see Section
5.1
. In particular, for smooth strongly convex TERM, we derive excess risk bounds that extend our (near-optimal) differentially private ERM bounds, since the TERM objective encompasses ERM in the limit.

Our last main contribution
is to specialize our theory and framework to
DP adversarial training
in Section
5.2
. In adversarial training, the goal is to train a model that has robust predictions against an adversary’s perturbations (with respect to some perturbation set
S
) of the feature data. This problem has gained a lot of attention in recent years, since it was first observed that neural nets can often be fooled by tiny, human-imperceptible perturbations into misclassifying images
[
GSS15
]
. However, the challenging task of ensuring such adversarial training is executed in a differentially private manner has received much less attention by researchers. Indeed, we are not aware of any prior works that have shown how to keep the adversarial training procedure differentially private and provided adversarial risk and runtime bounds. Perhaps the closest step in this direction is the work
[
PTH+20
]
, which provides a differentially private algorithm for training a classifier that is “certifiably robust,” in the sense that with high probability, the classifier’s predicted label is stable under small perturbations. However, our measure of adversarial robustness is different: we look at excess adversarial risk and provide bounds that depend explicitly on the privacy parameters. This allows for an interpretation of the tradeoffs between robustness, privacy, and runtime. Furthermore the noisy stochastic batch gradient descent algorithm of
[
PTH+20
]
is complicated to implement and does not come with runtime bounds.

Output Perturbation.

Our theory is built on the idea of
output perturbation
.
Conceptually, the output perturbation mechanism outputs

w_{\mathcal{A}}(X):=\tilde{w}(X)+b,

where
\tilde{w}
is the output of some non-private algorithm and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is some suitably chosen random noise vector.
Output perturbation has been studied in the differential privacy literature for many years
[
DKM+06
,
CMS11
,
ZZMW17
]
. In early works, which culminated in
[
DKM+06
]
, the method was introduced and proven to be differentially private. In
[
CMS11
]
, excess risk bounds for linear classifiers with strongly convex regularizers in the ERM and SCO settings are given via output perturbation. However, no practical implementation is provided. As a first step in the practical direction,
[
ZZMW17
]
shows how to implement output perturbation with gradient descent in the smooth ERM setting, providing excess empirical risk bounds (which they describe how to extend to excess population loss bounds) and runtime bounds. Their privacy analysis is tied to the particular non-private optimization method they use, which hinders their runtime potential and makes their analysis less transparent. We build on this line of work, by showing
how to implement output perturbation with
any (non-private) optimization method
, transforming it into a differentially private one
. In particular, by using accelerated and stochastic optimization methods, our method facilitates substantial runtime improvements. In addition, our analysis shows that output perturbation extends well beyond smooth ERM losses: e.g., we derive excess risk bounds for TERM and non-smooth losses.

Notation.

Recall that a function
h:\cal{W}\to\mathbb{R}
on some domain
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
convex
if
h(\lambda w+(1-\lambda)w^{\prime})\leq\lambda h(w)+(1-\lambda)h(w^{\prime})
for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
and all
w,w^{\prime}\in\cal{W}.
We say
h
is
\mu
-strongly convex
if
h(w)-\frac{\mu}{2}\|w\|_{2}^{2}
is convex. Also,
h
is
L
-Lipschitz
if
\|h(w)-h(w^{\prime})\|\leq L\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{2}
for all
w,w^{\prime}\in\cal{W}
and
\beta
-smooth
if
h
is differentiable and
\nabla h(w)
is
\beta
-Lipschitz.
The data universe
\mathcal{X}
can be be any set, and we often denote
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}.
For
R\geq 0,
define

\mathcal{M}_{R}:=\{F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}\,|\,~\forall X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}~\exists~w^{*}(X)\in\argmin_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(w,X),\|w^{*}(X)\|_{2}\leq R\},

and define
B(0,R)
to be the closed Euclidean ball of radius
R
centered at
0.

Throughout this paper, we will work with the following subfamilies of functions in
\mathcal{M}_{R}
:

\mathcal{G}_{L,R}:=\{F\in\mathcal{M}_{R}\,|\,F(\cdot,X)~\text{is}~L\text{-Lipschitz on}~B(0,R)~\text{and convex for all}~X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\};

\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}:=\{F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}\,|\,F(\cdot,X)~\text{is}~\beta\text{-smooth for all}~X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\};

\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}:=\{F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}\,|\,F(\cdot,X)~\text{is}~\mu\text{-strongly convex for all}X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\};

and

\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}:=\{F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}\,|\,F(\cdot,X)~\text{~is}~\beta\text{-smooth for all}~X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\};

(In fact, all of our results for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}
apply if
F
is merely
\mu
-strongly convex and/or
\beta
-smooth on
B(0,R),
rather than on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d},
but some of the discussion and proofs are cleaner when we use the above definitions.)
For
F\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R},
denote the condition number by
\kappa=\frac{\beta}{\mu}.
Also,
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM},\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM},...
are defined to be the subset of functions in
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R},\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R},...
that are of ERM form, i.e.
F(w,X)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(w,x_{i})
for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d},~X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
for some convex
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}.

For real-valued functions
a(\theta)
and
b(\phi)
, where
\theta
and
\phi
are vectors of parameters, we may write
a(\theta)\lesssim b(\phi)
if there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that
a(\theta)\leq Cb(\phi)
; alternatively, we may write
a=O(b).
If the same inequality holds up to a logarithmic function of the parameters instead of an absolute constant
C,
then we denote this relationship by
a=\widetilde{O}(b).
Finally, define the following constant that is used in our choice of Gaussian noise vector:
c_{\delta}:=\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{16\delta+1}-1}\right)}

[
ZWB+19
]
.

Roadmap.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In
section

2
, we describe our conceptual output perturbation algorithm and state excess risk and runtime bounds for four classes of general (i.e. non-ERM) functions, along with corresponding results for ERM. Then in
section

3
, we describe implementation details for our practical output perturbation method and establish runtime bounds. Next, we give population loss and runtime bounds for
\varepsilon
-differentially private SCO on the same four function classes in
section

4
. Finally, in
section

5
, we consider two applications of our results in machine learning: differentially private Tilted ERM (TERM)
[
LBSS20
]
and adversarial training.

2
Excess Risk Bounds for Conceptual Output Perturbation Method

2.1
Conceptual output perturbation algorithm

Assume for now that
F(\cdot,X)
in
eq.

2
is strongly convex, so that there is a unique minimizer
w^{*}(X)=\arg\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(w,X)
for all
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}.
Further,
we begin by assuming that we can exactly compute
w^{*}(X)
without regard to computational issues and present excess loss/risk bounds for the “conceptual” output perturbation algorithm

\mathcal{A}(X)=w_{\mathcal{A}}(X):=w^{*}(X)+z,

(2)

where
z
is random noise vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose distribution will be described below.
If
F
is not
\beta
-smooth, then we project
w_{\mathcal{A}}(X)
onto
B(0,R)
after adding noise since
w^{*}(X)+z
might not be in
B(0,R)
and we require
L
-Lipschitzness of
F
at
w_{\mathcal{A}}(X)
for our excess risk analysis (only in the non-smooth case). Denote the output of this latter algorithm by

\mathcal{A^{\prime}}(X)=w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X):=\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}(X)+z)=\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X)),

(3)

where
\Pi_{B(0,R)}(z):=\arg\min_{w\in B(0,R)}\|z-w\|_{2}=\frac{z}{\max\left\{1,\frac{\|z\|}{R}\right\}}.

Before describing the exact choice of noise, we introduce a notion that will be essential throughout our analysis.

Definition 1
.

Define the
L_{2}
sensitivity
of a (strongly convex in
w
) function
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\Delta_{F}:=\sup_{X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2}\|w^{*}(X)-w^{*}(X^{\prime})\|_{2},

where
w^{*}(X)=\argmin_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(w,X).

That is, the sensitivity of a function is the maximum
L_{2}
distance between the minimizers of that function over all pairs of data sets that differ in only one observation
x_{i}\neq x_{i}^{\prime}~(|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2)
. Note that
\Delta_{F}
clearly depends on the set
\mathcal{X}
(and on
n
and
d
), but we will hide this dependence in our notation, as we consider
\mathcal{X}
(and
n
and
d
) to be fixed. We sometimes may also just write
\Delta
if it is clear which function
F
we are referring to. Note that this definition is different from the notion of “sensitivity” that is sometimes used in the differential privacy literature (e.g.
[
DR14
]
), where “sensitivity of a function
g
” means
\sup_{X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{n},|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2}\|g(X)-g(X^{\prime})\|_{2}.
We prefer our notation since in our case, the function
g
of interest will almost always be
w^{*},
but we want to emphasize the dependence of
w^{*}
and
\Delta
on the underlying function
F.

2.2
Choice of Noise

There are many different choices of noise
z
to use in
eq.

2
and
eq.

3
for achieving differential privacy via output perturbation. Our choices are tailored to the setting of machine learning/optimization with convex Lipschitz (with respect to the Euclidean
L_{2}
norm) loss.

•

To achieve
(\varepsilon,0)
-privacy, we take
z
with the probability density function

p_{z}(t)\propto\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon\|t\|_{2}}{\Delta_{F}}\right)

(4)

for
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We have
\|z\|_{2}\sim\Gamma(d,\frac{\Delta_{F}}{\varepsilon}).

2
2

2

Here we say a random variable
Y\sim\Gamma(k,\theta)
if it has density
p_{Y}(y)={\displaystyle{\frac{1}{\Gamma(k)\theta^{k}}}y^{k-1}e^{-{\frac{y}{\theta}}}}
. Note
\mathbb{E}Y=k\theta
and
Var(Y)=k\theta^{2}.
To see that
\|z\|_{2}
has this distribution, one can write, for
s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
p_{\|z\|}(s)\propto\int_{\partial B(s)}\exp(-\|t\|\frac{\varepsilon}{\Delta_{F}})\propto e^{-s\frac{\varepsilon}{\Delta_{F}}}s^{d-1},
where
\partial B(s)
denotes the Euclidean sphere of radius
s
centered at the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and we used polar coordinates/surface measure of
\partial B(s)
to evaluate the integral.

•

For
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-privacy with
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
, we take

z=z_{\delta}\sim N(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d}),~\sigma^{2}=\left(\frac{c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{2}\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\Delta_{F}^{2},

(5)

where
c_{\delta}:=\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{16\delta+1}-1}\right)},
as proposed in
[
ZWB+19
]
. To distinguish between the output of the algorithm(s) with these different choices of noise, we shall denote
w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X)=w^{*}(X)+z_{\delta}
and
w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X)=\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}(X)+z_{\delta}).

Note that many other works (e.g.
[
ZZMW17
]
,
[
BST14
]
,
[
WYX17
]
,
[
FKT20
]
) instead use Gaussian noise with variance
\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{2\Delta^{2}\log(\frac{a}{\delta})}{\varepsilon^{2}}
, where
a>1
: this is the classical Gaussian mechanism introduced in
[
DKM+06
]
and improved in
[
DR14
]
. For
\widetilde{z}\sim N(0,\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}),
output perturbation is only
(\varepsilon,\delta)
differentially private for
\varepsilon\leq 1,
as explained in
[
ZWB+19
]
. Our choice of
\sigma
, borrowed from
[
ZWB+19
]
, has the following advantages:
\varepsilon>1
is allowed and in the comparable regime where
\varepsilon<1
,
\sigma\leq\widetilde{\sigma},
which leads to tighter excess risk upper bounds. On the other hand,
\delta\ll 1
is necessary for meaningful privacy guarantees, and usually
\delta\ll\frac{1}{n}
is desired, as discussed in the Introduction. So requiring that
\delta<1/2
is not restrictive indeed. Requiring
\varepsilon\leq 1
is much more restrictive since
\varepsilon>1
can still provide meaningful (albeit weaker) privacy guarantees if
\delta\ll 1
. However, if for some reason
\frac{1}{2}\leq\delta<1
and
\varepsilon\leq 1
is desired, then one may use the alternative form of Gaussian noise in our algorithms instead, while only affecting the excess risk bounds up to a logarithmic factor and not affecting runtime.
For the rest of this paper, we assume
\varepsilon>0
,
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).

We begin by establishing that our conceptual algorithm is differentially private (for
any

\varepsilon>0
).

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0,\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
function with
L_{2}
sensitivity
\Delta_{F}
and unique minimizer
w^{*}(X)=\argmin_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(w,X)
for all
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}.
The (conceptual) output perturbation algorithms
\mathcal{A}(X)=w_{\mathcal{A}}(X):=w^{*}(X)+z
and
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}(X)=w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X)=\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}(X)+z)
with noise given by density

p_{z}(t)\propto\begin{cases}\exp\left\{-\frac{\varepsilon\|t\|_{2}}{\Delta_{F}}\right\}&\mbox{if }\delta=0\\
\exp\left\{-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\|t\|_{2}^{2}}{\Delta_{F}^{2}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})^{2}}\right\}&\mbox{if }\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})\end{cases}

are
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private. Here
c_{\delta}:=\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{16\delta+1}-1}\right)}.

All proofs are relegated to the Appendix.

Note that if we do not have access to
\Delta_{F}
(or do not wish to compute it), then we can replace
\Delta_{F}
in the probability density function of
z
by it’s upper bound, given in
proposition

2.2
.
This will still ensure the algorithm is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-private and all of our excess risk bounds still hold.

In the remainder of this section, we will study particular classes of functions and aim to understand how well our output perturbation method performs at privately minimizing the (worst case over all functions in the class and data sets
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
) excess risk on these classes. We begin with strongly convex, Lipschitz functions in
section

2.3
and then add additional smoothness assumption in
section

2.4
.
We cover the non-strongly-convex case in
appendix

B
.
Our general strategy will be to estimate (upper bound) the sensitivity
\Delta_{F}
of functions in a given class and then use convexity, Lipschizness, and smoothness to upper bound the excess risk of our algorithm
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)
, in terms of
\Delta_{F}.

For the remainder of this paper, unless otherwise stated, we fix
n,d\in\mathbb{N}
and consider
\mathcal{X}
as a fixed arbitrary set
in any space.

2.3
Excess risk bounds for strongly convex, Lipschitz functions

In this subsection, assume our convex objective function
F
in
eq.

2
belongs to the following function class:

\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}=\{F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}\,|\,\exists F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}\,|\,F(w,X)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i})~\forall X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\}.

We say such
F
are “of ERM form.” Observe that for
F
of ERM form, i.e.
F(w,X)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i})
, we have that
F(\cdot,X)
is
\mu
-strongly convex for all data sets
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
if and only if
f(\cdot,x)
is
\mu
-strongly convex for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
. Moreover,
F(\cdot,X)
is
L
-Lipschitz for all data sets
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
if and only if
f(\cdot,x)
is
L
-Lipschitz for all
x\in\mathcal{X}.
Similarly,
F(\cdot,X)
is
\beta
-smooth for all data sets
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
if and only if
f(\cdot,x)
is
\beta
-smooth for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
3
3

3

Proof: The proof for all of the properties is similar. For concreteness, let’s consider the
\mu
-strong convexity condition. Assume
f(\cdot,x)
is
\mu
-strongly convex for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
(i.e.
g(w,x):=f(w,x)-\frac{\mu}{2}\|w\|_{2}^{2}
is convex in
w
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
) and let
w,w^{\prime}\in B(0,R),~X\in\mathcal{X}^{n},~\lambda\in[0,1].
Then denoting
G(w,X):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(w,x_{i})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i})-\frac{\mu}{2}\|w\|^{2},
we have

\displaystyle G(\lambda w+(1-\lambda)w^{\prime},X)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[f(\lambda w+(1-\lambda)w^{\prime},x_{i})-\frac{\mu}{2}\|w\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(\lambda w+(1-\lambda)w^{\prime},x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\lambda g(w,x_{i})+(1-\lambda)g(w^{\prime},x_{i})]

\displaystyle=\lambda G(w,X)+(1-\lambda)G(w^{\prime},X),

so that
G(\cdot,X)
is convex for all
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n},
and hence
F(\cdot,X)
is
\mu
-strongly convex for all
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}.
Conversely, suppose
f(\cdot,x)
is not
\mu
-strongly convex for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
. Then there exists
x\in\mathcal{X},~w,w^{\prime}\in B(0,R),~\lambda\in[0,1]
such that
g(\lambda w+(1-\lambda)w^{\prime},x)>\lambda g(w,x)+(1-\lambda)g(w^{\prime},x)
. Take
X=(x,\cdots,x)\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
to be
n
copies of the “bad” data point
x.
Then

\displaystyle G(\lambda w+(1-\lambda)w^{\prime},X)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(w,x_{i})

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\lambda g(w,x)+(1-\lambda)g(w^{\prime},x)]

\displaystyle=\lambda G(w,X)+(1-\lambda)G(w^{\prime},X),

which implies that
F(\cdot,X)
is not
\mu
-strongly convex for all
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}.

A similar argument easily establishes that the other assertions hold.

Our main result for this subsection is
corollary

2.2
, in which we establish
near-optimal
excess risk bounds for the subset of
smooth
functions that are contained in
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}.

As a first step towards deriving these excess risk upper bounds, we will need to estimate the sensitivity of
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}.
The following result generalizes
[
CMS11
, Corollary 8]
(beyond smooth linear ERM classifiers with bounded label space).

Proposition 2.2
.

For any
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM},\Delta_{F}\leq\frac{2L}{\mu n}.

In
appendix

F
, we prove that
the above sensitivity estimate for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
is tight
: there exists
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
such that
\Delta_{F}=\frac{L}{\mu n}
. However, there are some simple functions in
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
for which these estimates are not tight. For example, consider the following loss function:
f(w,x)=\frac{\beta}{2}\|Mw-x\|_{2}^{2}
for a symmetric positive definite
d\times d
matrix
M
with

\beta\mathbf{I}\succcurlyeq\beta M^{2}\succcurlyeq\mu\mathbf{I}
with
\mathcal{X}\subseteq B(0,R)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\beta\gg\mu
. Then
f
is
L:=2\beta R
-Lipschitz, and hence
F(w,X)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\beta}{2}\|Mw-x_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}.
In this case, by taking the gradient and computing
w^{*}(X)=M^{-1}\bar{X}
directly (where
\bar{X}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}
), we can obtain the more precise sensitivity estimate
\Delta_{F}\leq\frac{2R\sqrt{\kappa}}{n}
(where
\kappa=\frac{\beta}{\mu}
): for any
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
such that
|X\Delta X^{\prime}|=2
(say WLOG
x_{n}\neq x^{\prime}_{n}
), we have

\displaystyle\|w^{*}(X)-w^{*}(X^{\prime})\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|M^{-1}\bar{X}-M^{-1}\bar{X^{\prime}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|M^{-1}\|_{2}\|\bar{X}-\bar{X^{\prime}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\kappa}\left\|\frac{1}{n}(x_{n}-x^{\prime}_{n})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2R\sqrt{\kappa}}{n}.

Note that since
L=2\beta R
for this
F
, we have
\Delta_{F}\leq\frac{L\sqrt{\kappa}}{\beta n}=\frac{L}{\sqrt{\beta\mu}n}\ll\frac{2L}{\mu n}
.

Next, we establish excess risk upper bounds for our conceptual algorithm that depend on
\Delta_{F}
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
and
w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X)
be the perturbed solution defined in
eq.

3
.

a) If
\delta=0
and the noise
z
has the distribution in
eq.

4
, then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{L\Delta_{F}d}{\varepsilon}.

b) If
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and the noise
z
has distribution in
eq.

5
, then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq L\sqrt{d}\left(\frac{c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{2}\varepsilon}\right)\Delta_{F}\leq\frac{L\sqrt{d}\left(2c_{\delta}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)}{\sqrt{2}\varepsilon}\Delta_{F},

where
c_{\delta}=\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{16\delta+1}-1}\right)}.

Plugging the sensitivity estimates from
proposition

2.2
into
proposition

2.3
yields excess risk upper bounds for the class
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}:

Corollary 2.1
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}.

a) If
\delta=0,
then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{2L^{2}d}{\mu\varepsilon n}.

b) If
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right),
then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{d}(2c_{\delta}+\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon n}.

Moreover, if
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lesssim c_{\delta}
, then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{d}c_{\delta}}{\varepsilon n}.

Notice that in the practical regime
\delta\ll 1
, the condition
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lesssim c_{\delta}
is only restrictive if
\varepsilon>1
. In this regime, other algorithms (e.g. gradient descent output perturbation
[
ZZMW17
]
, noisy SVRG
[
WYX17
]
) that use the alternative form of Gaussian noise are not even differentially private in general, as shown in
[
ZWB+19
, Theorem 1]
. For
\varepsilon\leq 1
, the condition
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lesssim c_{\delta}
automatically holds for any
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
: indeed,
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\leq c_{\delta}
if
\varepsilon\leq 1
and
\delta\leq\frac{1}{9}.

Remark 2.1
.

Consider the trivial algorithm that outputs some fixed
w_{0}\in B(0,R)
(regardless of the input data or function), which has excess risk
\leq LR.
Then the above excess risk upper bounds (and indeed all other upper bounds in this paper) can be written as
\min\{LR,...\}
. Thus, the upper bounds given in
corollary

2.1
for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
are non-trivial only if
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}<1
(for
\delta=0
) or
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}<1
(for
\delta>0
).

Remark 2.2

(Suboptimality of output perturbation for non-smooth strongly convex ERM)
.

The private excess empirical risk lower bounds for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
are
\Omega\left(LR\min\left\{1,\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right\}\right)
for
\delta=0
and
\Omega\left(LR\min\left\{1,\frac{d}{(\varepsilon n)^{2}}\right\}\right)
for
\delta=o(\frac{1}{n})

[
BST14
]
.

In the ERM setting,
[
BST14
]
proposes algorithms that nearly achieve the excess risk lower bounds. For
\delta=0
, exponential mechanism with localization gives excess empirical risk of
O\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\log(n)\right)
, which is loose by a factor of
O\left(\log(n)\frac{L}{\mu R}\right)

[
BST14
]
. Compared to this upper bound, our ERM upper bound is loose by a factor of
O\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)
. However, our algorithm has some major advantages over their algorithm. First of all, our resulting population loss bounds beat theirs (by a logarithmic factor) (see
section

4
). Second, the algorithm proposed in
[
BST14
]
is impractical due to lack of implementation details for the localization/output perturbation step and large computational cost
4
4

4

The runtime of the exponential sampling step with input
\mathcal{W}_{0}
from the localization step (assuming the localization/output perturbation step is done) is
\widetilde{O}\left(\left(\frac{L}{\mu}\right)^{2}\frac{nd^{9}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\max\left\{1,\frac{L}{\mu}\right\}\right)
in the worst case; it improves by a factor of
d^{3}
if the convex set resulting from localization is in isotropic position
[
BST14
, Theorem 3.4]
. If one uses our methods to do that step efficiently, say via SGD (
section

3
), then the total runtime of their full algorithm (for general
B(0,R)
) would be
\widetilde{O}\left(\left(\frac{L}{\mu}\right)^{2}\frac{d^{9}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\max\{1,\frac{L}{\mu}\}+n^{2}d\right),
which is always larger than the runtime of our method.
. On the other hand, we will show in the next section that our algorithm can be implemented essentially as fast as any non-private algorithm.
For
\delta>0
, noisy SGD is an efficient practical algorithm, that can nearly (up to poly
\log(n)
factor) achieve the ERM lower bound in runtime
O(n^{2}d)
, but it does not apply for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
due to the suboptimal choice of Gaussian noise
[
BST14
, Section 2]
. In fact, their method requires
\varepsilon\leq 2\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}
, which restricts the level of excess risk that can be achieved. Furthermore, output perturbation has the benefit of simplicity and is easier (and computationally more efficient, as we will see in
section

3
) to implement, while also leading to population loss bounds (
section

4
) that outperform the bounds given in
[
BST14
, Appendix F]
. For these reasons, we consider our algorithm to be a competitive choice for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
.

Next, we show how to improve the above results and achieve near-optimal excess risk by adding a smoothness assumption.

2.3.1
Excess risk bounds for smooth, strongly convex, Lipschitz functions.

While
\beta
-smoothness does not improve the sensitivity estimates in
proposition

2.2
(as the sensitivity estimates are tight by
appendix

F
), it can be used to yield stronger excess risk bounds, which are nearly optimal in the ERM setting. Consider the smooth, strongly convex, Lipschitz function class

\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}:=\{F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}:F(\cdot,X)\text{~is}~\beta\text{-smooth}\text{~for all}~X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\}.

For
F\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}
, denote the condition number of
F
by
\kappa=\frac{\beta}{\mu}.
Our main result in this subsection is
corollary

2.2
.
For
F
in the ERM class
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
, we bound excess empirical risk by
O\left(\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right)
for
\delta=0
, assuming
d<\varepsilon n.
Our bound is
\widetilde{O}\left(\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{\varepsilon^{2}n^{2}}\right)
for
\delta>0
, assuming
d<\varepsilon^{2}n^{2}
. We obtain these results through the conceptual output perturbation algorithm
\mathcal{A}

without projection
, defined in
eq.

2
. As a first step, we obtain an analogue of
proposition

2.3
for
\beta
-smooth functions:

Proposition 2.4
.

Suppose
F(\cdot,X)
is
\beta
-smooth for all
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
with
L_{2}
sensitivity
\Delta_{F}.

a) If
\delta=0,
then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\frac{d(d+1)\Delta_{F}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leq\frac{\beta d^{2}\Delta_{F}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

b)If
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),
then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{\beta}{2}d\sigma^{2}=\frac{\beta}{4}\left(\frac{c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\Delta_{F}^{2},

where
c_{\delta}=\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{16\delta+1}-1}\right)}.

Combining
proposition

2.4
with
proposition

2.2
yields the following near-optimal excess risk guarantees:

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
w_{\mathcal{A}}(X)
be the perturbed solution defined in
eq.

2
.

a) Suppose
\delta=0
and the noise
z
has the distribution in
eq.

4
.

Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 4\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}.

b) Suppose
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and the noise
z
has the distribution in
eq.

5
. Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 4\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\leq 4\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}.

Furthermore, if
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lesssim c_{\delta}
, then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}c_{\delta}}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}.

Remark 2.3
.

Since
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
, by
corollary

2.1
, we obtain the following upper bounds on the excess risk of functions in
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim\min\left\{LR,\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{\varepsilon n},\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right\}
for
\delta=0
, and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim\min\left\{LR,\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{d}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon n},\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{\varepsilon})^{2}}{(\varepsilon n)^{2}}\right\}
for
\delta>0.
(Here
\mathcal{A}
refers to whichever conceptual algorithm yields smaller excess risk; it may or may not involve projection after perturbation.) Furthermore, as explained in earlier remarks, for
\delta\ll 1
or
\varepsilon\leq 1,
we have
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lesssim c_{\delta},
so that the upper bounds for
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
can be written more simply as
\widetilde{O}\left(\min\left\{LR,\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon},\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}\right)
and
\widetilde{O}\left(\min\left\{LR,\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n},\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{\varepsilon^{2}n^{2}}\right\}\right)
for
\delta=0
and
\delta>0
, respectively.

The excess risk lower bounds established in
[
BST14
]
for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
with
L\approx\mu R
also hold for
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}

if

\beta\approx\mu
(i.e.
\kappa=O(1)
) since their
L
-Lipschitz
\mu
-strongly convex hard instance is also
\mu
-smooth. Intuitively, it would seem that the class
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
with
\beta>\mu
is at least as hard to privately optimize than
\mathcal{H}^{ERM}_{\mu,\mu,L}
, but this is not proved in
[
BST14
]
. Our next result confirms that this intuition does indeed hold:

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
\beta,\mu,R,\varepsilon>0,
and
n,d\in\mathbb{N}
. Set
\mathcal{X}=B(0,R)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}.
For every
\varepsilon
-differentially private algorithm
\mathcal{A}
with output
w_{\mathcal{A}}
, there exists a data set
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
and a function
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
F\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
for
L=2\beta R
and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)=\Omega\left(LR\min\left\{1,\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right\}\right).

Moreover, if
\delta=o(1/n)
, then for every
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private algorithm
\mathcal{A},
there exists a data set
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)=\Omega\left(LR\min\left\{1,\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right\}\right).

For
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM},
there are several competing differentially private algorithms. A summary is given in
table

5
and
table

6
in
appendix

A
and a more detailed discussion is provided in
section

3
, after we derive runtime bounds. Here we note that we are not aware of any algorithm that is able to exactly achieve the lower bounds for
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
. The upper bounds in
[
BST14
]
for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
do not benefit from adding the smoothness assumption and therefore are still suboptimal by a factor of
O\left(\log(n)\frac{L}{\mu R}\right)
for the class
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
in general. Similarly, for
\delta>0,
both
[
BST14
]
and
[
WYX17
]
obtain excess risk upper bounds of
O\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{(\varepsilon n)^{2}}\log(n)\right)
for restricted ranges of
\varepsilon.
Thus,
when
\kappa\lesssim\log(n)
, our upper bounds for
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
are the tightest we are aware of
. For
\delta>0
, we also are the only method that is differentially private without restrictions on the
\varepsilon
. Furthermore, we show in
section

3
that
our runtime is also faster
than both of these competitors, and in
section

4
, we show that our method yields tighter population loss bounds.

We end this subsection by returning to the example introduced earlier and using the tight sensitivity bounds obtained earlier together with the results of this section to get tight excess risk bounds. Recall the function of interest is
f(w,x)=\frac{\beta}{2}\|Mw-x\|_{2}^{2}
for symmetric positive definite
M
with
\beta\mathbf{I}\succcurlyeq\beta M^{2}\succcurlyeq\mu\mathbf{I}
with
B(0,R)=\mathcal{X}=B_{2}(0,R)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For this function, we showed that
\Delta_{F}\leq\frac{2R\sqrt{\kappa}}{n}=\frac{L}{\sqrt{\beta\mu}n}
. Then applying
proposition

2.4
gives the excess risk upper bounds
O\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right)
for
\delta=0
and
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{(\varepsilon n)^{2}}\right)
for
\delta>0
. These nearly optimal (up to
\frac{L}{\mu R}
factor) upper bounds are tighter than those of any other competitor we are aware of. This example demonstrates how our general framework and analysis can result in tighter bounds than the general upper bounds given when specialized to specific functions or subclasses.

In the next subsection, we conduct a similar analysis under the weakened assumption that
F(\cdot,X)
is merely convex and
L
-Lipschitz.

2.4
Excess risk bounds for convex, Lipschitz functions

Consider the convex, Lipschitz function classes

\mathcal{G}_{L,R}=\{F\in\mathcal{M}_{R}\,|\ F(\cdot,X)\text{~is}~L\text{-Lipschitz on}~B(0,R)~\text{and convex for all}~X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\};

and

\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}:=\{F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}\colon\exists f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}~\text{s.t.}~F(w,X)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i})~\forall X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\}.

Observe that this definition implies that
f(\cdot,x)
must be convex for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
5
5

5

The proof of this fact is very similar to the proof of the comparable assertion for strong convexity, given in
section

2.3
.

Note that upper bounding the sensitivity in
proposition

2.2
requires strong convexity. So it is not immediately clear how to derive output perturbation excess risk bounds for functions in
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}.
To do so, we consider the regularized objective
F_{\lambda}(w,X):=F(w,X)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|w\|_{2}^{2}.
Note that
F_{\lambda}(\cdot,X)
is
(L+\lambda R)
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
and
\lambda
-strongly convex on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for any
\lambda>0
. Now we can run our conceptual output perturbation algorithm on
F_{\lambda},
and use our results from the previous section. Our conceptual (regularized) output perturbation algorithm is then defined as

\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}(X)=w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X):=\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X)+z_{\lambda}),

(6)

where
w^{*}_{\lambda}(X)=\arg\min_{w\in B(0,R)}F_{\lambda}(w,X)
and
z_{\lambda}
is a random noise vector with density

p_{z_{\lambda}}(t)\propto\begin{cases}\exp\left\{-\frac{\varepsilon\|t\|_{2}}{\Delta_{\lambda}}\right\}&\mbox{if }\delta=0\\
\exp\left\{-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\|t\|_{2}^{2}}{\Delta_{\lambda}^{2}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})^{2}}\right\}&\mbox{if }\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}).\end{cases}

Here
\Delta_{\lambda}:=\sup_{X,X^{\prime}\in|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2}\|w_{\lambda}^{*}(X)-w_{\lambda}^{*}(X^{\prime})\|_{2}
is the
L_{2}
sensitivity of
F_{\lambda}
and
c_{\delta}=\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{16\delta+1}-1}\right)}.

For
\delta>0
, note that the noise
z_{\lambda}\sim N(0,\sigma_{\lambda}^{2}I_{d})
, where
\sigma_{\lambda}=\left(\frac{c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{2}\varepsilon}\right)\Delta_{\lambda}.
Observe that by
proposition

2.2
,
\Delta_{\lambda}\leq\frac{2(L+\lambda R)}{\lambda}
if
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}
and
\Delta_{\lambda}\leq\frac{2(L+\lambda R)}{\lambda n}
if
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}.
If one does not have access to
\Delta_{\lambda}
or does not wish to compute it, for fixed
\lambda>0
one can replace
\Delta_{\lambda}
in the algorithm by these upper bounds and the following results in this section all still hold.

Our main result in this subsection is
proposition

2.7
, in which we establish upper bounds on the excess risk of the regularized output perturbation method for
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM},
of order
O\left(LR\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}}\right)
for
\delta=0
and
\widetilde{O}\left(LR\sqrt{\frac{d^{1/2}}{\varepsilon n}}\right)
when
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
.

First, by our choice of noise here in terms of the sensitivity of
F_{\lambda},
the same argument used to prove
proposition

2.1
establishes that this algorithm is differentially private:

Proposition 2.6
.

The regularized output perturbation algorithm
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}
described above is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private for any
\lambda>0
.

Next, we establish excess risk upper bounds for the classes
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}
and
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}
. The regularized output perturbation method defined in
eq.

6
with
\lambda
prescribed below (and outputs denoted by
w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}
and
w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}^{\delta}
for
\delta=0
and
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),
respectively) achieves each of the following excess risk bounds:

Set
\lambda=\frac{L}{R\sqrt{1+\frac{\varepsilon n}{d}}}.

a) Assume
\delta=0
and
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 8.5LR\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}}.

b) Assume
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.

Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 8.5LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}.

Furthermore, if
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lesssim c_{\delta},
then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim LR\left(\frac{d^{1/4}\sqrt{c_{\delta}}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon n}}\right).

Note that the above assumptions on the parameters are necessary in each case for the excess risk upper bounds to be non-trivial (i.e.
<LR
). Also, as noted in the previous subsection, the condition
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lesssim c_{\delta}
is only (formally) restrictive (requiring
\delta\ll 1
) if
\varepsilon>1.
For practical privacy assurances,
\delta\ll 1
is necessary, so this condition is easily satisfied.

The private excess empirical risk lower bounds for
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}
are:
\Omega\left(LR\min\left\{1,\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right\}\right)
for
\delta=0
, and

\Omega\left(LR\min\left\{1,\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}\right\}\right)
for
\delta=o(\frac{1}{n})

[
BST14
]
.
The exponential mechanism nearly achieves the lower bound for
\delta=0

[
BST14
]
. Thus, for
\delta=0
, we see that our above upper bound is loose by a factor of roughly
O\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}}\right)
in the non-trivial regime (
d<\varepsilon n
). For
\delta>0,
the noisy stochastic subgradient descent method of
[
BST14
]
nearly (up to a poly
\log(n)
factor) achieves the lower bound for
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM},
provided
\varepsilon\leq 2\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}.
Hence for
\delta>0
, our upper bound is loose by a factor of roughly
\frac{d^{1/4}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon n}}
in the non-trivial regime
\sqrt{d}<\varepsilon n
. However, we show in
section

4
that our algorithm again yields tighter population loss bounds than
[
BST14
]
for convex Lipschitz loss, despite the looser empirical loss bounds. This fact, in conjunction with the simplicity and faster runtime of our output perturbation method (discussed in
section

3
), and the freedom to choose
\varepsilon>2\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}
and
\delta>0~
makes our method an appealing alternative for both
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}
and
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}.

Next, we add the assumption of
\beta
-smoothess in order to derive tighter excess risk bounds.

2.4.1
Excess risk bounds for smooth, convex, Lipschitz functions.

The function class we consider now is:

\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}:=\{F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}\,|\ F(\cdot,X)~\text{is}~\beta\text{-smooth}~\text{for all}~X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}\}.

For such smooth, convex functions, we combine the techniques developed previously to derive excess risk bounds. More specifically, the regularization technique from
section

2.4
in conjunction with the descent lemma will yield
proposition

2.8
, which gives excess risk upper bounds of order

O\left(\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right)
for
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
when
\delta=0
and
O\left(\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right)
when
\delta\in(0,1/2)
.

While our ERM results are not optimal for the convex class
6
6

6

Recall that the excess risk lower bounds for
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
are
\Omega\left(\min\left\{LR,LR\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right\}\right)
and
\Omega\left(\min\left\{LR,LR\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}\right\}\right)
for
\delta=0
and
\delta>0
, respectively
[
BST14
]
.
, the speed and simplicity of our algorithm makes it the most practical choice for high-dimensional/large-scale problems, particularly when
\delta=0
and there are no other non-trivial algorithms that can be executed as fast as ours. We also are able to use the results proved in this section to get strong (in fact, the strongest we are aware of for
\delta=0
) private population loss bounds in
section

4
.

For any
F\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}
, consider the regularized objective
F_{\lambda}(w,X)=F(w,X)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|w\|_{2}^{2},
as defined earlier. Note that
F_{\lambda}(\cdot,X)
is
(\beta+\lambda)
-smooth in addition to being
(L+\lambda R)
-Lipschitz (on
B(0,R)
) and
\lambda
-strongly convex. We execute the following conceptual algorithm:

\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}(X)=w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X):=w^{*}_{\lambda}(X)+z_{\lambda}.

(7)

That is, we run output perturbation on the regularized objective (
without projection
). We can then apply the results from
section

2.3
to
F_{\lambda}
to obtain excess risk bounds for
F
. The method in
eq.

7
is differentially private by the same reasoning used to prove
proposition

2.1
. Our main results of this subsection are upper bounds on the excess risk of the regularized output perturbation method for
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
contained in the following proposition:

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}.
The regularized output perturbation method defined in
eq.

7
with
\lambda
prescribed below (and outputs denoted
w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}
or
w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}^{\delta}
for
\delta=0
and
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),
respectively)
achieves each of the following excess risk bounds:

a) Suppose
\delta=0,~\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{L}{R\beta}.
Set
~\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.

Then,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 48.5\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.

b) Suppose
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),~\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{L}{R\beta}.
Set
~\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.

Then,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 48.5\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.

In particular, if
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lesssim c_{\delta},
then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}c_{\delta}}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.

The hypotheses on given parameters are necessary to ensure that the upper bounds are non-trivial (i.e.
<LR
).

Remark 2.4
.

Since
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{L,R}
, we obtain the following upper bounds for
F\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
, by combining
proposition

2.8
with
proposition

2.7
:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim\min\left\{LR,LR\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2},\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right\}
for
\delta=0,

and
~\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim\min\left\{LR,LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}c_{\delta}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{1/2},\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}c_{\delta}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}\right\}
for
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\varepsilon\lesssim c_{\delta}^{2}.

For
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM},
there are a few existing differentially private algorithms (see
table

5
and
table

6
) besides the ones for
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}
(e.g.
[
BST14
]
, as discussed in
section

2.4
) which automatically apply. Among these methods that have practical implementation methods and reasonable runtime bounds (i.e. excluding the exponential mechanism) and apply to all functions in
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
, our method gives the tightest excess risk bounds for
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
when
\delta=0
. For
\delta=0
, the objective perturbation mechanism of
[
CMS11
]
is differentially private only for a strict subclass of
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
and (namely, functions in
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
satisfying the restrictive rank-one hessian assumption
\mbox{rank}(\nabla^{2}f(w,x))\leq 1
everywhere) but gives excess risk
\widetilde{O}\left(LR\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)\right)
for
\delta=0,
nearly matching the lower bound in the non-trivial parameter regime. Their method is purely conceptual, with no practical implementation process or runtime bounds. Excess risk bounds in
[
CMS11
]
are proved only for linear classifiers.
[
KST12
]
establishes nearly optimal excess risk bounds of
\widetilde{O}\left(LR\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)\right)
for rank-one hessian GLM functions in
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
when
\delta>0
via objective perturbation. They also do not provide a practical implementation procedure or runtime bounds. For
\delta>0,
the noisy SVRG method of
[
WYX17
]
results in excess risk of
\widetilde{O}\left(LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)\right),
making it the tightest known bound in this setting (and essentially matching the non-smooth risk bound of
[
BST14
]
, but achieving it in faster runtime). However, their results require
\beta
to be sufficiently large
7
7

7

This is stated explicitly as an assumption in
[
WYX17
, Thoerem 4.4]
and is also needed in order to guarantee their method is differentially private, by Theorem 4.3 in their work and the choices of algorithmic parameters if
\delta\ll 1.
. Therefore, if
\beta
is small, our method may be the most practical alternative for
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM},~\delta>0.

The other non-trivial excess risk bounds worth mentioning are those of
[
ZZMW17
]
. They give excess empirical risk upper bounds of
O\left(\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right)
and
O\left(\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right)
for
\delta=0
and
\delta>0
, respectively. Enhancing their upper bound for
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
by running the trivial algorithm whenever it does better than their algorithm, we match their upper bound for
\delta=0
whenever either
d\geq\varepsilon n
or
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\beta^{2}R^{8}}
(when either the first or third argument in the
\min\{...\}
in
remark

2.4
above is active). In the complementary case, when
1>\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}>\frac{L^{2}}{\beta^{2}R^{8}},
our non-smooth upper bound (from
proposition

2.7
) results in smaller excess risk than
[
ZZMW17
]
.
Furthermore, we do not require differentiability of
F
in order to achieve the upper bound in this regime (as the bound in
proposition

2.7
was obtained by merely assuming Lipschitzness and convexity). Similar remarks apply for
\delta>0.

Thus, our excess risk upper bounds dominate
[
ZZMW17
]
for all function classes where both of our algorithms apply, but we emphasize that our algorithm also applies to a much wider set of functions (non-smooth and non-ERM) and parameters (
\varepsilon>1
) than theirs. Furthermore, we will see that our algorithm offers significant runtime advantages over
[
ZZMW17
]
for
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
, potentially by even a bigger margin than for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
by using an accelerated stochastic gradient method instead of gradient descent (see
table

5
,
table

6
, and
section

C.2.1
for details).

In the next section, we describe how to efficiently implement our method in practice.

3
Efficient Implementation of Output Perturbation

In this section, we show that our conceptual output perturbation algorithm can be treated as a black box method: it can be efficiently implemented using any non-private optimization algorithm the user desires, transforming that method into a differentially private one without sacrificing runtime/computational cost. In particular, by choosing fast gradient/subgradient methods, we can achieve the conceptual upper bounds on excess risk from Section 2 in shorter runtime than any competing
\varepsilon
-differentially private algorithms that we are aware of. The idea and techniques we use for practical implementation of outptut perturbation are very similar in spirit to the conceptual algorithm of Section 2. At a high level, our method consists simply of finding an
approximate
minimizer of
F(\cdot,X)
(or
F_{\lambda}(\cdot,X)
in the non-strongly convex case) instead of an exact minimizer, and then adding noise, calibrated according to the sensitivity of the last iterate
w_{T}
(instead of
w^{*}
) to ensure privacy. Our
main result
in this section is Corollary
3.2
, which provides
near-optimal excess risk in near-linear-time
. We begin by studying strongly convex, Lipschitz loss functions.

3.1
Strongly convex, Lipschitz functions

Our general implementation method is described in
. Note that it accepts as input any non-private optimization algorithm and transforms it into a differentially private one, by adding noise to the approximate minimizer
w_{T}.
As a result, we are able to obtain runtimes as fast as non-private optimization methods while also achieving the conceptual excess risk bounds from Section 2 (by choosing
\alpha
appropriately).

Algorithm 1

Black Box Output Perturbation Algorithm for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}

algorithm]alg: black box sc

1:

Number of data points
n\in\mathbb{N},
dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
of data, non-private (possibly randomized) optimization method
\mathcal{M}
, privacy parameters
\varepsilon>0,\delta\geq 0
, data universe
\mathcal{X},
data set
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, function
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}
with
L_{2}
-sensitivity
\Delta_{F}\geq 0
, accuracy parameter
\alpha>0
with corresponding iteration number
T=T(\alpha)\in\mathbb{N}
(such that
\mathbb{E}[F(w_{T}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)]\leq\alpha
).

2:

Run
\mathcal{M}
for
T=T(\alpha)
iterations to ensure
\mathbb{E}F(w_{T}(X),X)-F(w^{*},X)\leq\alpha
.

3:

Add noise to ensure privacy:
w_{\mathcal{A}}:=w_{T}+\hat{z},
where the probability density function
p(\hat{z})
of
\hat{z}
is proportional to

\begin{cases}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon\|\hat{z}\|_{2}}{\Delta_{F}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}}\right)&\mbox{if }\delta=0\\
\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\|\hat{z}\|_{2}^{2}}{\left(\Delta_{F}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}\right)^{2}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)^{2}}\right)&\mbox{if }\delta>0\end{cases}.

4:

return

w_{\mathcal{A}}.

.
