Next, we establish excess risk upper bounds for the Black Box Algorithm on functions
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R},
which depend on the inputted accuracy parameter,
\alpha.

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
. Run
with arbitrary inputs and denote
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(X)=w_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(X):=\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X)),
where
w_{\mathcal{A}}(X)
is the output of
.

a) Let
\delta=0,~\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1
.
Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 2\sqrt{2}\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)+L\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}\right)\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)+\alpha.

In particular, setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n}\min\{\frac{1}{n},\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\}
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 9\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right).

b) Let
\delta>0,~\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 2\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n}+L\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}\right)\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)+\alpha.

In particular, setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n}\min\left\{\frac{1}{n},\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right\}
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 6\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right).

The assumptions on parameters in each case (e.g.
\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\leq 1
or
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\leq 1
for non-ERM) are necessary to ensure that the resulting upper bound is non-trivial. Note that with the choices of
\alpha
prescribed, the corresponding conceptual excess risk upper bounds from
corollary

2.1
are achieved. Furthermore, notice that it is not necessary to have a tight estimate of
\Delta_{F}
in order to achieve the excess risk bounds in
theorem

3.1
. Indeed, in
, one can replace
\Delta_{F}
by
\frac{2L}{\mu}
if
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}
or
\frac{2L}{\mu n}
if
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
(c.f.
proposition

2.2
) and the excess risk guarantees of
theorem

3.1
still hold. This is clear from the proof of
theorem

3.2
(see
section

C.1
).

Notice that
relies on an iterative algorithm for obtaining
w_{T}.
Next, we specialize our result to the case where a stochastic subgradient method (
algorithm

2
) is used to obtain
w_{T}
in
. Running the stochastic subgradient method with
\eta_{t}=\frac{2}{\mu(t+1)}
produces a point
\widehat{w}_{T}
such that
\mathbb{E}F(\widehat{w}_{T},X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\alpha
in
T=\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu\alpha}
stochastic gradient evaluations
[
Bub15
, Theorem 6.2]
. Since each iteration amounts to just one gradient evaluation of
f(w,x_{i})
(with runtime
d
), the resulting runtime of the full method is
O(dT).

Algorithm 2

Stochastic Subgradient Method (for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
)

1:

F(w,X)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i})\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
, data universe
\mathcal{X},
dataset
X=\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, iteration number
T\in\mathbb{N}
, step sizes
\{\eta_{t}\}~(t\in[T])
.

2:

Choose any point
w_{0}\in B(0,R).

3:

for

t\in[T]

do

4:

Choose
x_{i}\in X
uniformly at random, with replacement.

5:

w_{t}=\Pi_{B(0,R)}[w_{t-1}-\eta_{t-1}g(w_{t-1},x_{i})],
where
g(w,x_{i})\in\partial f(w,x_{i})
is a subgradient of
f
.

6:

end

for

7:

return

\widehat{w}_{T}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{2t}{T(T+1)}w_{t}.

Corollary 3.1
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
. Run
with Stochastic Subgradient Method (
algorithm

2
) with step size
\eta_{t}=\frac{2}{\mu(t+1)}
as the non-private
\mathcal{M}
.

a) Let
\delta=0,\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n}\min\left\{\frac{1}{n},\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right\}
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 9\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}

in
T=2n\max\left\{n,\frac{\varepsilon}{d}\right\}
stochastic gradient evaluations. The resulting runtime is
O(\max\{dn^{2},\varepsilon n\}).

b) Let
\delta>0,\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n}\min\left\{\frac{1}{n},\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right\}
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 6\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}

in
T=2n\max\left\{n,\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{d}(2c_{\delta}+\sqrt{\varepsilon})}\right\}
stochastic gradient evaluations. The resulting runtime is
O\left(\max\left\{dn^{2},n\sqrt{d\varepsilon}\right\}\right).

In the ERM setting, the only competing algorithms we are aware of for non-smooth objective (
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
) are the exponential sampling + localization and noisy SGD methods of
[
BST14
]
. Consider the ERM case with
\delta=0
: in
[
BST14
]
, they show that exponential sampling + localization achieves excess risk bounds of
O\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\log(n)\right)
. While these bounds are tighter than ours in most parameter regimes, their runtime is generally much slower. An implementation method for the output perturbation/localization step of their algorithm is not provided, but the second step of their algorithm alone (consisting of the exponential mechanism initialized with a smaller set
\mathcal{W}_{0}\subset B(0,R)
obtained from localization) has runtime
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\frac{nd^{6}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\max\left\{1,\frac{L}{\mu}\right\}\right),

provided
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is in isotropic position
; in the worst case (if
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is non-isotropic), then runtime would slow by an additional factor of
O(d^{3})

[
BST14
]
. Implementing the first step (output perturbation/localization) through methods similar to our own would increase the runtime in
[
BST14
]
by an additional additive term comparable to our total runtime. On the other hand, our output perturbation method has much smaller runtime of
O(dn^{2})
when stochastic subgradient method is used.
Furthermore, our resulting population loss bounds match (in fact, beat by a logarithmic factor) the population loss bounds of
[
BST14
]
for this class (see
section

4
). Thus, our method is a more practical choice in most parameter regimes.

For
\delta>0,
the noisy stochastic subgradient algorithm of
[
BST14
]
achieves runtime of
O(n^{2}d),
while achieving excess empirical risk of
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{(\varepsilon n)^{2}}\right),
which is better for most parameter regimes than ours. However, their method requires
\varepsilon\leq 2\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
in order to be differentially private, whereas our method is private for all
\varepsilon>0,
giving more flexibility to further reduce excess risk (while sacrificing some privacy) by choosing larger
\varepsilon.
Furthermore, we will show in
section

4
that we can obtain tighter
population loss
bounds than those established in
[
BST14
]
, suggesting that our algorithm is a competitive alternative, even in the
\delta>0
setting.

Next we analyze the Black Box
applied to the subclass of
\beta
-smooth functions
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}\subset\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}
.

Smooth, strongly convex Lipschitz functions.

We begin by stating an analogue of
theorem

3.1
for
\beta
-smooth loss functions.

Theorem 3.2
.

Suppose
F\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}.
Run
on
F\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}
with arbitrary inputs.

a) Let
\delta=0
and assume
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.

Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 4\beta\left(\frac{L}{\mu n}+\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+\alpha.

In particular, setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n^{2}}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 26\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}.

b) Let
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and assume
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1
. Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 2\beta\left(\frac{L}{\mu n}+\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+\alpha.

In particular, setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n^{2}}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 13.5\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}.

Note that the prescribed choices of
\alpha
ensure the excess risk upper bounds from
corollary

2.2
are attained (up to constant factors).

With
\alpha
and
\mathcal{M}
chosen appropriately in
, we can use
theorem

3.2
to ensure that the excess risk bounds for
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
(
corollary

2.2
) are attained in as little runtime as any non-private optimization method. In particular, plugging Nesterov’s Accelerated Gradient Descent (AGD) method
[
Nes14
]
or, in the ERM setting, an accelerated stochastic method such as Catalyst
[
LMH15
]
or Katyusha
[
AZ18
]
into
yields
the fastest differentially private algorithm we are aware of
, while also achieving
near-optimal
(up to
\kappa
factor) excess empirical risk for
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
.

Algorithm 3

Katyusha

1:

F(w,X)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(w,x_{i})+\psi(w):=G(w,X)+\psi(w),
where each
g(\cdot,x_{i})
is convex,
\beta
-smooth and
\psi
is
\mu
-strongly convex, data universe
\mathcal{X},
dataset
X=\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, iteration number
T\in\mathbb{N}
(corresponding to accuracy parameter
\alpha
).

2:

m:=2n.

3:

\tau_{2}:=\frac{1}{2},\tau_{1}:=\min\{\frac{\sqrt{m\mu}}{\sqrt{3L}},\frac{1}{2}\},\gamma:=\frac{1}{3\tau_{1}\beta}.

4:

y_{0},z_{0},\widetilde{w}_{0}:=w_{0}.

5:

for

t=0,1,...T-1

do

6:

\mu_{t}=\nabla G(\widetilde{w}_{t},X).

7:

for

j=0,1,...m-1

do

8:

k:=(tm)+j.

9:

w_{k+1}=\tau_{1}z_{k}+\tau_{2}\widetilde{w}_{t}+(1-\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})y_{k}.

10:

\widetilde{\nabla}_{k+1}=\mu_{t}+\nabla g(w_{k+1},x_{i})-\nabla g(\widetilde{w_{s}},x_{i})
for
i
chosen uniformly from
[n].

11:

z_{k+1}:=\argmin_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|z-z_{k}\|_{2}^{2}+\langle\widetilde{\nabla}_{k+1},z\rangle+\psi(z)].

12:

y_{k+1}=\argmin_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\frac{3\beta}{2}\|y-w_{k+1}\|_{2}^{2}+\widetilde{\nabla}_{k+1},y\rangle+\psi(y)].

13:

end

for

14:

\widetilde{w}_{t+1}=(\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(1+\gamma\mu)^{j})^{-1}(\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(1+\gamma\mu)^{j}y_{tm+j+1}).

15:

end

for

return

\widetilde{w}_{T}.

For
F\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM},
we input an accelerated stochastic method as
\mathcal{M}
in
. In particular, the Katyusha method of
[
AZ18
]
, outlined in
algorithm

3
, provides the following guarantee:

Theorem 3.3
.

[
AZ18
, Theorem 2.1 simplified]

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R},~F(w)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(w)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}(w)+\psi(w),
where each
g_{i}
is convex and
\beta
-smooth,
\psi
is
\mu
-strongly convex, and
\kappa:=\frac{\beta}{\mu}.
Then running
algorithm

3
for
T=O\left(\left(n+\sqrt{n\kappa}\right)\log\left(\frac{F(w_{0})-F(w^{*})}{\alpha}\right)\right)
stochastic gradient iterations returns a point
\widetilde{w}_{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
F(\widetilde{w}_{T})-F(w^{*})\leq\alpha.

Clearly, given any
F\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
and any
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, we can write
F(w,X)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(w,x_{i})+\psi(w),
where
\psi(w):=\frac{\mu}{2}\|w\|_{2}^{2}

is
\mu
-strongly convex,
g(\cdot,x_{i})=f(\cdot,x_{i})-\psi(\cdot)
is convex and
(\beta-\mu)
-smooth (hence
\beta
-smooth) for all
x_{i}\in X.

Combining
theorem

3.3
with
theorem

3.2
, we obtain the following:

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}.
Take
\mathcal{M}
to be Katyusha and
T=O\left(\left(n+\sqrt{n\kappa}\right)\log\left(\frac{LR}{\alpha}\right)\right)
as inputs in
.

a) Let
\delta=0,\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n^{2}}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}
gives

~\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 26\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}

in
T=\widetilde{O}(n+\sqrt{n\kappa})
stochastic gradient evaluations. The resulting runtime is

\widetilde{O}(d(n+\sqrt{n\kappa})).

b) Let
\delta>0,\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n^{2}}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 13.5\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}

in
T=\widetilde{O}(n+\sqrt{n\kappa})
stochastic gradient evaluations. The resulting runtime is

\widetilde{O}(d(n+\sqrt{n\kappa})).

Remark 3.1
.

Since
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
, by
corollary

2.1
, we obtain the following upper bounds on the excess risk of functions in
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim\min\{LR,\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{\varepsilon n},\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\}
for
\delta=0
; and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim\min\{LR,\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{d}(c+\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon n},\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{p(c+\sqrt{\varepsilon})^{2}}{(\varepsilon n)^{2}}\}
for
\delta>0
for
\delta>0.
Furthermore, the runtime for these enhanced upper bounds are (up to log terms) as stated above in
corollary

3.2
, since
obtaining the excess risk bounds of
corollary

3.1
via Katyusha only affects the choice of
\alpha,
which is inside of a
\log(\cdot)
in the expression for the iteration complexity of Katyusha.

As noted in
section

2.3.1
(and as can be seen in
table

5
and
table

6
), there are several existing differentially private methods for
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}.
However, output perturbation, when implemented with Katyusha (or another accelerated stochastic method such as Catalyst) achieves the
fastest runtime of all of these
.

In addition to faster runtime, our method is more widely applicable than many other methods (e.g. for
\delta>0,
our method is the only one that applies for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
, as can be seen in
table

6
). Let us discuss these differences in a bit more detail. Consider
\delta=0.
First, the objective perturbation method achieves excess empirical risk of
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right),
but requires twice differentiability and (crucially)
\mbox{rank}(\nabla_{w}^{2}f(w,x))\leq 1
everywhere
[
CMS11
]
. Formally, their excess risk bounds are only proved for linear classifiers, but it does not seem that this restriction is necessary. In addition, it is a purely conceptual method: no practical implementation procedure or runtime estimate is provided. On the other hand, our output perturbation method does not have any of these restrictions and can be easily implemented in very fast runtime
\widetilde{O}\left(d(n+\sqrt{n\kappa})\right)
via Katyusha.
Recall that the excess empirical risk upper bound for
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
in
[
BST14
]
is
O\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\log(n)\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right)
for
\delta=0,
achieved by the exponential mechanism with localization. Smoothness does not improve this bound to the best of our knowledge. Assuming
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}<1,
so that our bound and that of
[
BST14
]
are both non-trivial, our upper bound for
\delta=0
(
remark

2.3
) is tighter when
\kappa<\log(n)
or when
1\lesssim\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\log(n)
. In addition, our method is much simpler and runtime is much faster.
The last method we mention for
\delta=0
is the gradient descent-based output perturbation method of
[
ZZMW17
]
. They give excess risk bounds for
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}

that match those given in
corollary

2.2
. In light of
remark

2.3
, however, there are some parameter regimes where our excess risk bounds are smaller than theirs. First, ignoring constants, we see that when
d>\varepsilon n
our upper bound (
remark

2.3
) is the trivial upper bound
LR
. When
d<\varepsilon n
so that
LR>\min\left\{\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{\varepsilon n},\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n})^{2}\right\}
, there are two subcases: if
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}
, then the resulting upper bound is
\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n})^{2}.
Finally, when
\kappa
is very large so that
\frac{1}{\kappa}<\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1
, then our upper bound becomes
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}
. On the other hand,
[
ZZMW17
]
has excess risk
O\left(\kappa(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n})^{2}\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\right)
for
\delta=0.
If we trivially enhance their algorithm to instead perform the trivial algorithm whenever it yields smaller excess risk (
LR
), then our upper bound matches theirs in the first two cases mentioned above and is superior in the last case. Furthermore, the last case (very large
\kappa
) is also where we dominate in terms of runtime by allowing for stochastic acceleration. By comparison, their runtime is
\widetilde{O}(nd\kappa).

Similar remarks apply when
\delta>0
. If
\varepsilon>1,
then the method of
[
ZZMW17
]
is not even differentially private, so our excess risk dominates theirs trivially. Assume
c_{\delta}\lesssim\sqrt{\varepsilon}
(this is guaranteed automatically when
\varepsilon\leq 1
and
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
). In this case, our upper bound is non-trivial iff
\frac{c_{\delta}\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}<1,
in which case our upper bound reduces to
O\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}c_{\delta}}{\varepsilon n}\right)\min\left\{1,\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}c_{\delta}}{\varepsilon n}\right)\right\}\right).
On the other hand, the upper bound of
[
ZZMW17
]
, which is differentially private only when
\varepsilon\leq 1
, is
O\left(\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}{\varepsilon^{2}n^{2}}\right).
By
[
ZWB+19
]
, our logarithmic factor
c_{\delta}\leq\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}
, so our bound is at least as good as
[
ZZMW17
]
when
\kappa\frac{c_{\delta}\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}<1.
On the other hand, when
1\leq\kappa\frac{c_{\delta}\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}
, our upper bound is also tighter since then
\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}(\frac{c_{\delta}\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n})<\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}{\varepsilon^{2}n^{2}}.

For
\delta>0
, the noisy SGD algorithm of
[
BST14
]
yields excess risk
O(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{(\varepsilon n)^{2}}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\log(\frac{n}{\delta})^{2}).
Assuming
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\lesssim 1
so that both of our bounds are non-trivial, our upper bound is tighter when
\kappa\lesssim\log(\frac{n}{\delta})^{2}.
Furthermore, our algorithm allows for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0,
whereas their results only apply for
\varepsilon\leq 2\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}.
The noisy SVRG method of
[
WYX17
]
is faster than the method of
[
BST14
]
, with runtime of
\widetilde{O}\left(d(n+\sqrt{\kappa}n)\right),
while achieving comparable excess risk. However, their method requires small
\varepsilon=O\left(\frac{\kappa}{n^{2}}\right)
to be differentially private, which is quite restrictive. By contrast, our method provides the flexibility for arbitrarily large
\varepsilon,
providing a full continuum of accuracy-privacy tradeoff options.
Finally, objective perturbation also applies for
\delta>0

[
KST12
]
, but again comes with additional restrictions. In addition to rank-one hessian and GLM assumption, they also require
n\geq d^{2}
and
\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}=\widetilde{O}(1).
Moreover, no implementation procedure or runtime bounds are given.

In Appendix
C.2
, we use regularization to efficiently implement our conceptual regularized output perturbation method when the objective function is merely convex.

4
From Empirical Loss to Population Loss

Recent works
[
BST14
,
BFTGT19
,
FKT20
]
have established tight bounds on the expected population loss of
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-private stochastic optimization algorithms for
\delta>0
. However, the important case of
\delta=0,
which provides the strongest privacy guarantee, has largely been overlooked, except for
[
BST14
]
and (in the smooth, strongly convex case)
[
ZZMW17
]
.

We show that our simple output perturbation algorithm results in expected excess population loss bounds that improve the expected excess population loss bounds in
[
BST14
]
and can be executed in substantially less runtime.
Moreover, for the smooth, strongly convex function class where
[
ZZMW17
]
gives expected excess population loss bounds, our bounds are tighter (or matching, in certain parameter regimes) and are achieved in less runtime. Our method also works for
\delta>0,
where it serves as a simple, flexible, efficient alternative which, unlike the other algorithms proposed so far, also allows for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0.
The
main result
of this section is stated in Corollary
4.2
.

Change of Notations.
In this section, we change some notation for further clarity and introduce some new definitions. Let the data have some (unknown) distribution
x\sim\mathcal{D}.
We are given a sample (i.i.d.) data set
X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n}.

We will denote the normalized empirical loss
\widehat{F}(w,X):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i})
and the
population loss

F(w,\mathcal{D}):=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}f(w,x).

We want to understand how output perturbation performs in privately minimizing the
expected excess population loss
, defined at a point
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

F(w,\mathcal{D})-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(w,\mathcal{D})=F(w,\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D}),

(8)

where we denote a parameter that minimizes the population loss by
w^{*}(\mathcal{D}).
That is,

w^{*}(\mathcal{D})\in\argmin_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(w,\mathcal{D})

To avoid any ambiguity, in this section, we will denote the minimizer of the
empirical
loss for a given data set
X
by
\widehat{w}(X).
Denote the output of the differentially private output perturbation algorithm by
w_{\mathcal{A}}(X)=\widehat{w}(X)+z
where z is the same noise we used before. Our approach is to extend our ERM results to population loss bounds using the algorithmic stability-based techniques introduced in
[
BE02
]
and applied by
[
HRS16
]
and
[
BFTGT19
]
.

We begin with a definition and lemma from
[
BE02
]
that will be very useful for us.

Definition 2
.

(Uniform stability) Let
\alpha>0.
A (possibly randomized) algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{X}^{n}\to B(0,R)
is
\alpha
-uniformly stable (w.r.t loss function
f
) if for any pair of data sets
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
differing by at most one point (i.e.
|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2
), we have

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}[f(\mathcal{A}(X),x)-f(\mathcal{A}(X^{\prime}),x)]\leq\alpha.

Informally, the definition says that a uniformly stable algorithm is one that for similar datasets, produces outputs with similar loss values on average.

Lemma 4.1
.

(
[
BE02
]
)

Let
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an
\alpha
-uniformly stable algorithm w.r.t. loss function
f
and let
X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n}.
Then

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}[F(\mathcal{A}(X),\mathcal{D})-\widehat{F}(\mathcal{A}(X),X)]\leq\alpha.

Combining
lemma

4.1
with our excess empirical risk bounds from Section 2 for each ERM function class will enable us to upper bound the population loss when running output perturbation on the empirical loss
\widehat{F}
. We begin with the strongly convex, Lipschitz class.

4.1
Strongly convex, Lipschitz functions

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
f(\cdot,x)
be
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
and
\mu
-strongly convex for all
x\in\mathcal{X},
let
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Let
X
be a data set of size
n
drawn i.i.d. according to
\mathcal{D}.
Run the conceptual output perturbation algorithm (
eq.

3
) on the empirical loss
\widehat{F}(w,X)
to obtain
w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X).

a) If
\delta=0
and
\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)\leq 1
, then

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right).

b) If
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)\leq 1,
then

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right).

In order to prove
proposition

4.1
, we will need to estimate the uniform stability of the output perturbation algorithm:

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
f(\cdot,x)
be
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
and
\mu
-strongly convex for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
. Then the conceptual output perturbation algorithms
\mathcal{A}
(
eq.

2
) and
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
(
eq.

3
) are
\alpha
-uniformly stable for
\alpha=\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu n}.

This lemma is proved in
appendix

D
. Combining this with
lemma

4.1
enables us to bridge the gap between our empirical loss results and the population loss bound claimed above. See
appendix

D
for details.

By using stochastic subgradient descent (SGD,
algorithm

2
) to approximately minimize the empirical objective
\widehat{F},
as described in
section

3.1
, we can attain the expected excess population loss bounds stated in
proposition

4.1
efficiently:

Corollary 4.1
.

Let
f(\cdot,x)
be
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
and
\mu
-strongly convex for all
x\in\mathcal{X},
let
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Let
X
be a data set of size
n
drawn i.i.d. according to
\mathcal{D}.
Run
\mathcal{A}=

on
\widehat{F}
with
\mathcal{M}
as the stochastic subgradient method (
algorithm

2
) with step sizes
\eta_{t}=\frac{2}{\mu(t+1)}
and
~T,\alpha
as prescribed below. Denote the output of this algorithm by
w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X).

a) Suppose
\delta=0,~\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n}\min\{\frac{1}{n},\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\}
and
T=2\max\left\{n^{2},\frac{n\varepsilon}{d}\right\}
implies

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{5}{n}+9\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right).

The runtime of this method is
O(\max\{dn^{2},\varepsilon n\}).

b) Suppose
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),~\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n}\min\left\{\frac{1}{n},\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right\}
and
T=2n\max\left\{n,\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{d}(2c_{\delta}+\sqrt{\varepsilon})}\right\}
implies

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{5}{n}+6\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right).

The resulting runtime is
O\left(\max\left\{dn^{2},n\sqrt{d\varepsilon}\right\}\right).

The optimal non-private expected excess population loss for
\mu
-strongly convex,
L
-Lipschitz
f
is
O(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n})

[
HK14
]
. So if
\delta=0
and
d\lesssim\varepsilon,
then we match the optimal non-private rate and in effect get “privacy for free.” Likewise, for
\delta>0
, if
\sqrt{d}c_{\delta}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lesssim\varepsilon,
then we get privacy for free.

As noted earlier, neither
[
BFTGT19
]
, nor
[
FKT20
]
give expected excess population loss bounds for
\delta=0
. The only private population loss bound we are of for with
\delta=0
is
O\left(\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\right)\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\sqrt{\log(n)}+\frac{1}{n}\right)\right)
in
[
BST14
, Theorem F.2]
, obtained by exponential sampling + localization.
Thus, we beat their loss bound by a logarithmic factor. More importantly, however, is our runtime advantage. As noted above, our runtime is
O\left(\max\left\{n^{2}d,\varepsilon n\right\}\right)
for
\delta=0.

By contrast, the runtime of the exponential sampling + localization method of
[
BST14
]
is generally much larger:
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu^{2}}d^{9}\frac{n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\max\{1,\frac{L}{\mu}\}\right)
in the (non-isotropic) worst case
[
BST14
, Theorem 3.4, Lemma 4.2]
.

For
\delta>0,

[
FKT20
, Algorithm 3, Sec. 5.1]
gives an efficient phased ERM algorithm that can nearly (up to a
O\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)
factor) achieve the optimal (by
[
BFTGT19
]
) private rate of
O\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{d}{(\varepsilon n)^{2}}\right)\right)
in the same runtime as our method:
\widetilde{O}(n^{2}d)
. However, this algorithm requires
\varepsilon\leq 2\log(\frac{1}{\delta})
to be differentially private since they use the suboptimal form of Gaussian noise at each iteration, whereas we provide the flexibility of choosing any
\varepsilon>0.
Additionally, our method is arguably much simpler to implement in practice.

The noisy SGD method of
[
BST14
]
also results in a population loss bound of
O\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}\log^{2}\left(\frac{n}{\delta}\right)\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)\right)
for
\delta>0
, which is looser than our bound by a factor of
\log^{2}\left(\frac{n}{\delta}\right).
This method requires
\varepsilon\leq 2\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
to be differentially private and has the same runtime as our algorithm.

Smooth, strongly convex, Lipschitz functions.

Adding the assumption of
\beta
-smoothness results in tighter,
near-optimal
private population loss bounds. We begin with loss bounds for the conceptual output perturbation algorithm
eq.

2
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
f(\cdot,x)
be
\mu
-strongly convex,
L
-lipschitz, and
\beta
-smooth for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
with condition number
\kappa=\beta/\mu,
let
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Let
X
be a data set of size
n
drawn i.i.d. according to
\mathcal{D}.
Run
eq.

2
on the empirical loss
\widehat{F}(w,X).

a) If
\delta=0
and
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1,
then

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{2}{n}+4\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right).

b) If
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1,
then

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{2}{n}+4\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right).

With the smoothness assumption, we can also use Katyusha instead of SGD for implementation and obtain faster runtimes:

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
f(\cdot,x)
be
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
,
\mu
-strongly convex, and
\beta
-smooth for all
x\in\mathcal{X},
let
\varepsilon>0.
Let
X
be a data set of size
n
drawn i.i.d. according to
\mathcal{D}.
Run
\mathcal{A}=

on
\widehat{F}(w,X)
with
\mathcal{M}
as Katyusha with
T=O\left((n+\sqrt{n\kappa})\log\left(\frac{LR}{\alpha}\right)\right)
and
\alpha
as prescribed below.

a) Suppose
\delta=0
and
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n^{2}}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}
results in a point
w_{\mathcal{A}}(X)
such that

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{5}{n}+26\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right)

in
T=\widetilde{O}(n+\sqrt{n\kappa})
stochastic gradient evaluations. The resulting runtime is
\widetilde{O}(d(n+\sqrt{n\kappa})).

b) Suppose
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then setting

\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n^{2}}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}
results in a point
w_{\mathcal{A}}(X)
such that

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{5}{n}+13.5\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right)

in

T=\widetilde{O}(n+\sqrt{n\kappa})
stochastic gradient evaluations.
The resulting runtime is
\widetilde{O}(d(n+\sqrt{n\kappa})).

Remark 4.1
.

Since the smooth, strongly convex, Lipschitz loss function class is a subset of the strongly convex, Lipschitz loss function class, we obtain by
proposition

4.1
the following upper bounds on the expected excess population loss of smooth, strongly convex, Lipschitz functions:
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\lesssim\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)+\min\left\{LR,\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{\varepsilon n},\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right\}
for
\delta=0
; and
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\lesssim\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)+\\
\min\left\{LR,\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n},\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right\}
for
\delta>0.
Furthermore, the runtime for these enhanced upper bounds are (up to log terms) as stated above:
\widetilde{O}\left(d(n+\sqrt{\kappa n})\right).
This is because obtaining the expected excess population loss bounds of
proposition

4.1
via Katyusha (on the empirical loss) only affects the choice of
\alpha
(in
section

3
), which is inside of a
\log(\cdot)
in the expression for the iteration complexity of Katyusha.

The non-private statistically optimal population loss is
O(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n}),
as for non-smooth. Hence, if
\delta=0,
we get “privacy for free” whenever
\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\lesssim n
or
d\lesssim\varepsilon
. For
\delta>0
, we get “privacy for free” whenever
\frac{\kappa dc_{\delta}^{2}}{\varepsilon}\lesssim n
or
\sqrt{d}c_{\delta}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\lesssim\varepsilon.

For
\delta=0
,
[
ZZMW17
]
presents an expected excess population loss bound of
O\left(\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}+\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\right)
(via gradient descent with output perturbation), which is achieved in runtime
\widetilde{O}(dn\kappa).
Thus, their loss bound matches ours whenever
\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)<1
and is otherwise inferior by
remark

4.1
; their runtime is also slower than ours, especially when
\kappa
is large. Besides
[
ZZMW17
]
, the only other private population loss bound we are aware of for the smooth, strongly convex, Lipschitz class with
\delta=0
is the one from exponential sampling + localization in
[
BST14
]
, which does not benefit additionally from smoothness, to the best of our knowledge. Therefore, our method provides the tightest
(\varepsilon,0)
-differentially private excess population loss bounds that we are aware of and runs in less time than any competing algorithm.

For
\delta>0,~\varepsilon\leq 2\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right),
the recent work of
[
FKT20
, Section 5]
attains the optimal bound of
O\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\sqrt{d\log({\frac{1}{\delta})}}}{\varepsilon n}\right)\right)
with
an efficient algorithm, provided that
\kappa\kappa\leq\frac{n}{\log(n)}
.
By contrast,
our algorithm does not require any restrictions on
\kappa
or
\varepsilon,
which make it an attractive alternative
, particularly for ill-conditioned problems or problems where only a modest amount of privacy is desired, but strong (perhaps non-private) expected excess population loss is needed.

In Appendix
D.2
, we extend our algorithm to convex loss functions via regularization.

5
Applications to Private Tilted ERM and Private Adversarial Training

In this section, we specialize our developed theory to two specific applications in machine learning: differentially private tilted ERM (TERM) and adversarial training. As discussed in the Introduction (
section

1
), both of these problems are of great practical importance in modern machine learning and yet there is no known efficient differentially private algorithm for solving either of them while providing excess risk guarantees. Using our general methodology and results from Sections 2-4, we will address these gaps and obtain excess loss and runtime bounds for privately optimizing TERM and adversarial objectives via output perturbation.

5.1
Differentially Private Tilted Empirical Risk Minimization

Consider the tilted ERM (TERM) problem

\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[F_{\tau}(w,X):=\frac{1}{\tau}\log\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{\tau f(w,x_{i})}\right)\right],

where
\tau>0
is a given constant (see e.g.
[
KB72
,
PR11
,
CS14
,
CSS16
,
KF17
,
LBSS20
]
and the references therein for the applications of this loss). It is easy to show that
as
\tau\xrightarrow{}0,F_{\tau}(w,x)\xrightarrow{}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i})
, so this extends the classical ERM framework in the limit. It also encompasses the max loss (
F_{max}(w,X)=\max\{f(w,x_{1}),...,f(w,x_{n}\}
) for instance, by letting
\tau\xrightarrow{}\infty.
More generally, a benefit of the TERM framework is that it allows for a continuum of solutions between average and max loss, which, for example, can be calibrated to promote a desired level of fairness in the the machine learning model
[
LBSS20
]
. Another interpretation of TERM is that as
\tau
increases, the variance of the model decreases, while the bias increases. Thus,
\tau
can also be tuned to improve the generalization of the model via the bias/variance tradeoff. In what follows, we specialize our developed theory to the TERM objective function.

Our
main result
in this section is
theorem

5.2
, which gives
near-optimal excess risk
for smooth, strongly convex TERM with an efficient algorithm.

5.1.1
Strongly convex, Lipschitz, twice differentiable
f(\cdot,x)

First, we show that if
f
is “nice enough,” then
F_{\tau}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R},
so that the excess risk bounds proved earlier hold; we will also refine these results to show how excess risk depends on the parameter
\tau>0.
Assume
f(\cdot,x)
is a
\mu
-strongly convex, twice differentiable,
L
-Lipschitz loss function for all
x\in\mathcal{X}.
Then we compute:

\nabla_{w}F_{\tau}(w,X)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}(\tau,w)\nabla_{w}f(w,x_{i}),

(9)

where
~v_{i}(\tau,w):=\frac{e^{\tau f(w,x_{i})}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}e^{\tau f(w,x_{j})}}.

It follows easily from
eq.

9
that (for
\tau>0
) if
f(\cdot,x)
is
L
-Lipschitz for all
x_{i}\in X
then
F_{\tau}(\cdot,X)
is
L
-Lipschitz. Furthermore,
F_{\tau}(\cdot,X)
is
\mu
-strongly convex:

Lemma 5.1
.

[
LBSS20
, Lemma 3]

Let
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
,
\tau>0
. Assume
f(\cdot,x_{i})
is twice-differentiable and
\mu
-strongly convex for all
x_{i}\in X.
Then
F_{\tau}(\cdot,X)
is twice-differentiable and
\mu
-strongly convex.

It follows from
[
LBSS20
, Lemma 3]
and
eq.

9
that
F_{\tau}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}.
Then combining
eq.

9
and
lemma

5.1
with
proposition

2.2
implies that the
L_{2}
sensitivity of
F_{\tau}
is bounded as
\Delta_{F_{\tau}}\leq\frac{2L}{\mu}
for all
\tau>0
and even as
\tau\to\infty
. If, in addition,
f
is bounded on
B(0,R)\times\mathcal{X}
, then we also have the following estimate of the sensitivity of
F_{\tau}
:

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\tau>0.
Suppose
f(\cdot,x)
is
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
(where
\|w^{*}(X)\|\leq R
) and
\mu
-strongly convex for all
x\in\mathcal{X}.
Moreover, assume
a_{R}\leq f(w,x)\leq A_{R}
for all
w\in B(0,R)
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}.
Then

\Delta_{F_{\tau}}\leq\frac{2L}{\mu}\min\left\{1,\frac{C_{\tau}}{n}\right\}

where
C_{\tau}:=e^{\tau(A_{R}-a_{R})}.

Observe that requiring boundedness of
f
on
B(0,R)\times\mathcal{X}
is not very restrictive: indeed, it is automatic if
f
is continuous and
\mathcal{X}
is compact (e.g. if input data is normalized), by the extreme value theorem. Also, note that as
\tau\to 0,
we recover the sensitivity estimate
\frac{2L}{\mu n}
of
proposition

2.2
for
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}^{ERM}.
On the other hand, as
\tau
increases,
\Delta_{F_{\tau}}
increases. This is intuitive since when
\tau\to\infty
,
F_{\tau}\to F_{max},
so the behavior of the loss is determined by only the worst case data point, which increases the sensitivity.

lemma

5.2
implies that running our conceptual output perturbation algorithm with projection
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
(
eq.

3
) on
F_{\tau}
ensures
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-privacy and also gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F_{\tau}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F_{\tau}(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{2L^{2}C_{\tau}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)~\text{if}~\delta=0,

and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F_{\tau}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F_{\tau}(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}L^{2}C_{\tau}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)~\text{if}~\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),

by
proposition

2.3
if
f
is bounded on
B(0,R)\times\mathcal{X}.
(Note: throughout this section, we denote
w^{*}(X)\in\argmin F_{\tau}(w,X).
)

Moreover, we can achieve these risk bounds efficiently:

Theorem 5.1
.

Assume
f(\cdot,x)
is
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
and
\mu
-strongly convex and twice differentiable for all
x\in\mathcal{X}.
Assume further that
a_{R}\leq f(w,x)\leq A_{R}
for all
w\in B(0,R)
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}.
Then for any
\tau>0,
running
on
F_{\tau}
with
\mathcal{M}
as the subgradient method yields

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F_{\tau}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F_{\tau}(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 9\frac{L^{2}C_{\tau}}{\mu}\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}

in
T=\frac{2}{C_{\tau}}\max\{\frac{n^{2}}{C_{\tau}},\frac{\varepsilon n}{d}\}
gradient evaluations of
F_{\tau}
and runtime
O\left(\frac{n^{2}d}{C_{\tau}}\max\left\{\frac{\varepsilon}{d},\frac{n}{C_{\tau}}\right\}\right)
for
\delta=0.
For
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),
we get

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F_{\tau}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F_{\tau}(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 9\frac{L^{2}C_{\tau}}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}

in runtime
O\left(\frac{nd}{C_{\tau}}\max\left\{\frac{n^{2}}{C_{\tau}},\frac{\varepsilon n}{\sqrt{d}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})}\right\}\right).
Here
C_{\tau}=e^{\tau(A_{R}-a_{R})}
.

These results follow by plugging the sensitivity estimate from
lemma

5.2
into the proof of
theorem

3.1
with
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}C_{\tau}}{\mu n}\min\left\{\frac{C_{\tau}}{n},\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right\}
for
\delta=0
and
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}C_{\tau}}{\mu n}\min\left\{\frac{C_{\tau}}{n},\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right\}
for
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and then recalling the iteration complexity
T=\left\lceil\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu\alpha}\right\rceil
of the subgradient method. Alternatively, one could use the stochastic TERM algorithm described in
[
LBSS20
, Appendix C]
as the non-private input
\mathcal{M}
in
. However, no iteration complexity/runtime bounds are given for that method in
[
LBSS20
]
.

5.1.2
Smooth, strongly convex, Lipschitz, twice differentiable
f(\cdot,x)

If we assume additionally that
f(\cdot,x_{i})
is
\beta
-smooth for all
x_{i},i\in[n]
, then
F_{\tau}(w,X)
is
\beta_{\tau}
-smooth with
\beta_{\tau}
given in the following:

Lemma 5.3
.

Assume
f(\cdot,x_{i})
is
\beta
-smooth and
L
-Lipschitz for all
i\in[n].
Then for any
\tau>0,
the TERM objective
F_{\tau}(\cdot,X)
is
\beta_{\tau}
-smooth for
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
, where
\beta_{\tau}:=\beta+L^{2}\tau.

See Appendix
E.1
for the proof. It follows by combining the above lemmas that if
f
is twice-differentiable,
\beta
-smooth,
\mu
-strongly convex, and
L
-Lipschitz (i.e.
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}
), then
F_{\tau}\in\mathcal{H}_{\left(\beta+2L^{2}\tau\right),\mu,L,R}.
Hence by
corollary

2.2
, upon running the conceptual output perturbation algorithm
eq.

2
, we obtain:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F_{\tau}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F_{\tau}(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 4\kappa_{\tau}\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}~\text{if}~\delta=0,

and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F_{\tau}(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F_{\tau}(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 4\kappa_{\tau}\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}~\text{if}~\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),

where
\kappa_{\tau}:=\frac{\beta_{\tau}}{\mu}=\frac{\beta+2L^{2}\tau}{\mu}
is the condition number of
F_{\tau}.
These bounds hold even for unbounded
f
and as
\tau\to\infty;
however, the non-smooth bounds from the previous subsection, which also apply here, would be tighter as
\tau\to\infty,
since then
\kappa_{\tau}\to\infty.

If, in addition,
f
is bounded on
B(0,R)\times\mathcal{X},
then plugging the estimate for
\Delta_{F_{\tau}}
from
lemma

5.2
into
proposition

2.4
yields:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F_{\tau}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F_{\tau}(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 4\kappa_{\tau}\frac{L^{2}C_{\tau}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}~\text{if}~\delta=0,

and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F_{\tau}(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F_{\tau}(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 4\kappa_{\tau}\frac{L^{2}C_{\tau}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}~\text{if}~\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),

With
\beta
-smoothness, we can also use Nesterov’s Accelerated Gradient Descent (AGD,
algorithm

4
) as
\mathcal{M}
in
to speed up runtime and obtain:

Algorithm 4

Nesterov’s (Projected) Accelerated Gradient Descent (AGD)

1:

F(w,X)\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}
, data universe
\mathcal{X},
dataset
X=\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, iteration number
T\in\mathbb{N}
.

2:

Choose any point
w_{0}\in B(0,R).

3:

for

t\in[T]

do

4:

y_{t}=\Pi_{B(0,R)}[w_{t-1}-\frac{1}{\beta}\nabla F(w_{t-1},X)].

5:

w_{t}=\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}-1}{\sqrt{\kappa}+1}\right)y_{t}-\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}-1}{\sqrt{\kappa}+1}y_{t-1}.

6:

7:

end

for

8:

return

\widehat{w}_{T}=y_{T}.

Theorem 5.2
.

Assume
f(\cdot,x)
is
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
,
\beta
-smooth,
\mu
-strongly convex and twice differentiable for all
x\in\mathcal{X}.
Assume further that
a_{R}\leq f(w,x)\leq A_{R}
for all
w\in B(0,R)
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}.
Then for any
\tau>0,
running
with
\mathcal{M}
as AGD on
F_{\tau}
yields

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F_{\tau}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F_{\tau}(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 26\kappa_{\tau}\frac{L^{2}C_{\tau}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}

if
\delta=0,
and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F_{\tau}(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F_{\tau}(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 13.5\kappa_{\tau}\frac{L^{2}C_{\tau}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}

if
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}).

Here
C_{\tau}=e^{\tau(A_{R}-a_{R})}
and
\kappa_{\tau}=\frac{\tau L^{2}+\beta}{\mu}.
Both of these excess risk bounds are realized in runtime
\widetilde{O}(dn\sqrt{\kappa_{\tau}}).

To derive
theorem

5.2
, we simply plug the estimate of
\Delta_{F_{\tau}}
from
lemma

5.2
into the proof of
theorem

3.2
and set

\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n^{2}}\begin{cases}\min\left\{\kappa_{\tau}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},C_{\tau}^{2}\right\}&\mbox{if}~\delta=0\\
\min\left\{\kappa_{\tau}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},C_{\tau}^{2}\right\}&\mbox{if}~\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}).\end{cases}

Then recall the iteration complexity
T=\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{\kappa_{\tau}})
of AGD (where we hide a logarithmic factor that depends on
\alpha
) for obtaining
w_{T}
such that
F_{\tau}(w_{T},X)-F_{\tau}(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\alpha.

5.2
Differentially Private Adversarially Robust Learning

In recent years, adversarial attacks on neural networks have raised significant concerns on reliability of these methods in critical applications. Adversarial attacks are input examples to a machine learning model crafted by making small perturbations to legitimate inputs to mislead the network. These adversarial examples lead to highly confident, but incorrect outputs; see e.g.
[
SZS+14
,
GSS15
,
PMJ+16
,
EEF+18
,
MFF16
]
and the references therein. A natural approach to training a model that is robust to such adversarial attacks is to solve the following adversarial training problem
[
MMS+18
,
ZYJ+19
,
NSH+19
]
:

\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}F(w,X+\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y}).

(10)

Here
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
contains the feature data,
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n}
is the corresponding label/target vector (e.g.
y_{i}\in\{0,1\}
for a binary classification task), and
S
is a set of permissible adversarial perturbations. As discussed in
section

1
, solving
eq.

10
with a practical differentially private algorithm to ensure privacy, robustness, and computational speed simultaneously is an important open problem. Indeed, we are not aware of any works that provide privacy, robustness (“excess adversarial risk”), and runtime guarantees for solving
eq.

10
. In this section, we illustrate how our methods and results developed so far can be easily applied to establish excess adversarial risk bounds (robustness guarantees) and runtime bounds via output perturbation.

5.2.1
Notation and preliminaries

Let
D=(X,\mathbf{y})=((x_{1},y_{1}),\cdots,(x_{n},y_{n}))\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\cal{Y}\right)^{n}
be a given training data set. Assume that the adversarial perturbation set
S
is convex and compact with
L_{2}
diameter
\rho

and that the adversary chooses
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})\in S^{n}
corresponding to the training examples
((x_{1},y_{1}),\cdots,(x_{n},y_{n})).
The following definition is for notational convenience:

Definition 3
.

For a loss function
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\mathcal{X}+S)^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n},
and dataset
D=(X,\mathbf{y})\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n},
denote the function of the weights and perturbations corresponding to
F
by

H_{D}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times S^{n}\to\mathbb{R},~H_{D}(w,\mathbf{v}):=F(w,X+\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y}).

We make the following additional assumptions:

Assumption 1
.

H_{D}(\cdot,\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}
for all
\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}
and all
D\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\cal{Y}\right)^{n}.

Assumption 2
.

H_{D}(w,\cdot)
is continuous and concave for all
w\in B(0,R)
and all
D\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\cal{Y}\right)^{n}.

Note that
2
is a standard assumption in the min-max literature
[
NSH+19
,
LJJ20
]
. In addition, problems with a discrete adversary set
S
can be transformed into problems that satisfy
2
(see
[
RHL+20
]
). Together with
1
, it ensures the existence of a saddle point (see definition below) and enables us to (approximately) find the saddle point and implement our output perturbation method efficiently (see
section

5.2.3
). However, it is not strictly necessary for the results for conceptual output perturbation that we present in
section

5.2.2
. Next, we recall a basic notion from min-max optimization:

Definition 4
.

For
\alpha\geq 0,
say a point
(\widehat{w},\widehat{\mathbf{v}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times S^{n}
is an
\alpha
-saddle point
of a convex (in
w
)-concave (in
\mathbf{v}
) function
H(w,\mathbf{v})
if

\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}H(\widehat{w},\mathbf{v})-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}H(w,\widehat{\mathbf{v}})\leq\alpha.

Observe that by
1
,
2
, and convexity and compactness of
B(0,R)
and
S
, there exists at least one
\alpha
-saddle point point
(\widehat{w},\widehat{\mathbf{v}})\in B(0,R)\times S^{n}
of
H_{D}
for any
\alpha\geq 0.
Also, if we denote

G_{D}(w):=\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}H_{D}(w,\mathbf{v})=\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}F(w,X+\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y}),

which is in
\mathcal{F}_{\mu,L,R}
(by
1
and compactness of
S^{n}
), and

w^{*}(D):=\argmin_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{D}(w)=\argmin_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S}H_{D}(w,\mathbf{v})\in B(0,R),

then for any
\alpha
-saddle point
(\widehat{w},\widehat{\mathbf{v}})
of
H_{D},
we have:

\displaystyle G_{D}(\widehat{w})-G_{D}(w^{*}(D))

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}H_{D}(\widehat{w},\mathbf{v})-\min_{w\in B(0,R)}H_{D}(w,\widehat{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle+\min_{w\in B(0,R)}H_{D}(w,\widehat{\mathbf{v}})-\max_{v\in S^{n}}H_{D}(w^{*}(D),\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha+0,

because

\displaystyle\min_{w\in B(0,R)}H_{D}(w,\widehat{\mathbf{v}})-\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}H_{D}(w^{*}(D),\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}\min_{w\in B(0,R)}H_{D}(w,\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle-\min_{w\in B(0,R)}\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}H_{D}(w,\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=0,

by Sion’s minimax theorem
[
Sio58
]
.

For a model
w_{\mathcal{A}}
trained on loss function
F
(by some randomized algorithm
\mathcal{A}
), the measure of adversarial robustness that we will consider is:

Definition 5
.

Let
w_{\mathcal{A}}
be the output of a randomized algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for solving
eq.

10
. Define the
excess adversarial risk
of
\mathcal{A}
by

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}},X+\mathbf{v},y)-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}F(w,X+\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}G_{D}(w_{\mathcal{A}})-G_{D}(w^{*}(D)).

In what follows, we aim to quantify the tradeoffs between excess adversarial risk, privacy, and runtime for output perturbation. We begin by considering the conceptual output perturbation algorithm, in which we assume that we can compute an exact (
\alpha=0
) saddle point of
H_{D}(w,\mathbf{v}).

5.2.2
Conceptual output perturbation for differentially private adversarial training

The key step towards applying our theory is to notice that by
1
, the
L_{2}
sensitivity
\Delta_{G_{D}}
of
G_{D}(w)
is bounded according to
proposition

2.2
:

\Delta_{G_{D}}\leq\frac{2L}{\mu}
for general
F
and
\Delta_{G_{D}}\leq\frac{2L}{\mu n}
if
F
(and hence
G_{D}
) is of ERM form. To clarify further, note that if
F(w,X,y)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i},y_{i})
is of ERM form, then
f(\cdot,x_{i}+v_{i},y_{i})
is
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-Lipschitz for all
(x_{i},y_{i})\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
and all
v_{i}\in S
by
1
and the discussion in the first footnote of
section

2.3
. It follows that
G_{D}(w)=\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i}+v_{i},y_{i})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}(w),
where
g_{i}(w):=\max_{v_{i}\in S}f(w,x_{i}+v_{i},y_{i})
is
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-Lipschitz by the following:

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times S
be such that
h(\cdot,v)
is
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mu
-strongly convex in
w
for all
v\in S.
Assume further that
S
is compact and that
h(w,\cdot)
is continuous for all
w\in B(0,R).
Then
g(w):=\max_{v\in S}h(w,v)
is
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
and
\mu
-strongly convex.

Our conceptual output perturbation algorithm is described in
algorithm

5
. The fact that it is differentially private follows from applying
proposition

2.1
to
G_{D}
and the sensitivity estimate for
G_{D}
stated above.

Algorithm 5

Conceptual Output Perturbation for DP Adversarial Training

1:

Number of data points
n\in\mathbb{N},
dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
of feature data, non-private (possibly randomized) optimization method
\mathcal{M}
, privacy parameters
\varepsilon>0,\delta\geq 0
, feature data universe
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and label universe
\mathcal{Y},
data set
D=(X,\mathbf{y})\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}
, convex compact perturbation set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
of diameter
\rho\geq 0,
loss function
F(w,X,y)
that is
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
and
\mu
-strongly convex in
w
and concave in
\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}.

2:

Solve
\min_{w\in B(0,R)}\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}F(w,X+\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y})
to obtain a saddle point
(w^{*}(D),v^{*}(D)).

3:

Add noise to ensure differential privacy:

w_{\mathcal{A}}(D):=\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}(D)+z)
, where the density
p_{z}(t)
of
z
is given as:

p_{z}(t)\propto\begin{cases}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon\|t\|_{2}}{\Delta}\right)&\mbox{if }\delta=0\\
\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\|t\|_{2}^{2}}{\Delta^{2}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)^{2}}\right)&\mbox{if }\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right),\\
\end{cases}

and
\Delta:=\frac{2L}{\mu n}

By the results in
section

2.3
and
section

2.3.1
, we can obtain excess adversarial risk bounds for strongly convex-concave, Lipschitz
F
with or without the
\beta
-smoothness (in
w
) assumption. Here we state the results for
\beta
-smooth
F,
which is a simple consequence of
corollary

2.2
:

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
D=(X,\mathbf{y})\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}
be a dataset
and let
\varepsilon>0,\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Assume that
S
is a convex compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and that the ERM loss function
F
is such that
1
and
2
hold (see
definition

3
). Furthermore, assume that
H_{D}(\cdot,\mathbf{v})
is
\beta
-smooth for all
\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}
.

a) If
\delta=0,
then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}G_{D}(w_{\mathcal{A}})-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{D}(w)\leq 4\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}.

b) If
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),
then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}G_{D}(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta})-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{D}(w)\leq 4\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}.

Observe that the stated assumptions on
F
and
H
above are stronger than what is required to obtain
proposition

5.1
. Indeed, instead of assuming strong convexity, Lipschitzness, and smoothness of
H_{D}(w,v),
it is enough to assume strong convexity, Lipschitzness, and smoothness of
G_{D}(w)=\max_{\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}}F(w,X+\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y})
for all
D=(X,\mathbf{y})\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}
. Moreover, concavity is not necessary: all we need is the existence of a saddle point of
H_{D}
for any
D\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}.

proposition

5.1
describes the tradeoffs between excess adversarial risk, privacy,
n,
and
d
: as the privacy level increases (i.e. as
\varepsilon
and
\delta
shrink) and the dimension
d
increases, the guaranteed excess adversarial risk (robustness) bound increases; and vice-versa. Furthermore, as the number of samples
n
increase, it is possible to obtain more favorable adversarial excess risk and privacy guarantees for any fixed
d
. Note also that similar results could be written down in the non-smooth case as consequences of
corollary

2.1
. Likewise, one can obtain excess adversarial risk bounds in the non-strongly convex case by using the regularization technique and invoking
proposition

2.7
or
proposition

2.8
. We omit the details here.

In the next subsection, we present a practical, efficient algorithm for implementing differentially private adversarial training with runtime bounds for attaining the above robustness guarantees.

5.2.3
Efficiently Implementing Output Perturbation for Private Adversarial Training

In order to practically implement the output perturbation mechanism, we make the following additional assumption:

Assumption 3
.

H_{D}(w,\cdot)
is
\beta_{v}
-smooth as a function of
\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}
for all
w\in B(0,R)
and all
D=(X,\mathbf{y})\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}.

Then
eq.

10
is a smooth (in
w
and
v
), strongly convex-concave min-max problem and there are efficient non-private algorithms for solving such problems
[
NS07
,
ADS+20
,
LJJ20
]
. In
algorithm

6
, we give a differentially private Black Box Algorithm tailored to the special min-max structure of the adversarial training objective. We then instantiate the Algorithm with the near-optimal (in terms of gradient complexity) non-private algorithms of
[
LJJ20
]
and provide upper bounds on the runtime for privately optimizing the adversarially robust model to match the levels of excess risk obtained in
proposition

5.1
(and/or the corresponding non-smooth version, which matches the bounds in
corollary

2.1
.)

Algorithm 6

Black Box Output Perturbation Algorithm for Implementing DP Adversarial Training

1:

Number of data points
n\in\mathbb{N},
dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
of feature data, non-private (possibly randomized) optimization method
\mathcal{M}
, privacy parameters
\varepsilon>0,\delta\geq 0
, feature data universe
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and label universe
\mathcal{Y},
data set
D=(X,\mathbf{y})\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}
, convex compact perturbation set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
of diameter
\rho\geq 0,
loss function
F(w,X,\mathbf{y})
that is
L
-Lipschitz and
\mu
-strongly convex in
w
on
B(0,R)
and concave in
\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}
, accuracy parameter
\alpha>0
with corresponding iteration number
T=T(\alpha)\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(w_{T},v_{T})
is an
\alpha
-saddle point of
H_{D}(w,\mathbf{v}).

2:

Run
\mathcal{M}
for
T=T(\alpha)
iterations to obtain an
\alpha
-saddle point
(w_{T},v_{T})
of
H_{D}(w,\mathbf{v})=F(w,X+\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y}).

3:

Add noise to ensure differential privacy:

w_{\mathcal{A}}(D):=\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w_{T}+\widehat{z})
, where the density of
\widehat{z}
is given as:

p_{\widehat{z}}(t)\propto\begin{cases}\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon\|t\|_{2}}{\Delta+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}}\right)&\mbox{if }\delta=0\\
\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\|t\|_{2}^{2}}{\left(\Delta+\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}\right)^{2}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)^{2}}\right)&\mbox{if }\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right),\\
\end{cases}

and
\Delta:=\frac{2L}{\mu n}.

4:

return

w_{\mathcal{A}}(D).

The exact same arguments used in Section 3 show that
algorithm

6
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private and that the following excess adversarial risk guarantees hold:

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
D=(X,\mathbf{y})\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}
be a dataset
and let
\varepsilon>0,\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Assume that
S
is a convex compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and that the ERM loss function
F
is such that
1
,
2
, and
3
hold (see
definition

3
). Furthermore, assume that
H_{D}(\cdot,v)
is
\beta
-smooth for all
\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}
.
Run
\mathcal{A}=

algorithm

6
on
F
with arbitrary inputs.

a) Let
\delta=0
and
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}G_{D}(w_{\mathcal{A}})-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{D}(w)\leq 4\beta\left(\frac{L}{\mu n}+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{\mu}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+\alpha.

In particular, setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n^{2}}\min\{\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\}
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}G_{D}(w_{\mathcal{A}})-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{D}(w)\leq 26\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}.

b) Let
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1
. Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}G_{D}(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta})-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{D}(w)\leq 2\beta\left(\frac{L}{\mu n}+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{\mu}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+\alpha.

In particular, setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n^{2}}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}
gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}G_{D}(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta})-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{D}(w)\leq 13.5\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}.

The theorem follows immediately from
theorem

3.2
. Note that there is no explicit dependence on the size
\rho
of the adversary’s perturbation in the above adversarial risk bounds. However, the parameters
L,\beta,\beta_{v},
and
\mu
(which we assumed to be fixed) may depend on
\rho.
Furthermore, the iteration complexity and runtime depends on
\rho
as we shall see below. There is also no dependence on
\beta_{v}
in the above bounds, but this parameter does influence runtime as we will also see. Finally, note that a result similar to
theorem

5.3
for
non-smooth
(in
w
)
F
can easily be written down as a direct consequence of
theorem

3.1
.

Next, we instantiate our Black Box Algorithm with
\mathcal{M}
= “Minimax-APPA”
[
LJJ20
, Algorithm 4]
), which combines an inexact accelerated proximal point method to minimize
\max_{\mathbf{v}}F(w,X+\mathbf{v},\mathbf{y})
with Nesterov’s accelerated gradient method for solving the outer max problem. We refer the interested reader to
[
LJJ20
]
for further details about the algorithm. We restate two results from
[
LJJ20
]
, translated into our notation.

Proposition 5.2
.

(
[
LJJ20
, Thm 5.1/Cor 5.2]
)

Assume
H(\cdot,v)
is
\mu
-strongly convex,
\beta
-smooth, (with condition number
\kappa=\beta/\mu
) for all
v\in S,
where
S
is a closed convex set with
L_{2}
diameter
\rho.
Assume
H(w,\cdot)
is
\beta_{v}
-smooth and concave as a function of
v\in S
for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Then

1. Minimax-APPA returns an
\alpha
-saddle point of
H
in at most
T=\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\kappa\beta_{v}}{\alpha}}\rho\right)
total gradient evaluations.

2. If, in addition,
H(w,\cdot)
is
\mu_{v}
-strongly concave, then Minimax-APPA returns an
\alpha
-saddle point in at most
T=\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{\kappa\kappa_{v}})
gradient evaluations, where
\kappa_{v}=\frac{\beta_{v}}{\mu_{v}}
is the condition number.

Using the above result, together with
theorem

5.3
, we obtain the following:

Corollary 5.1
.

Let
D=(X,\mathbf{y})\in(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{n}
be a dataset
and let
\varepsilon>0,\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Assume that
S
is a convex compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of
L_{2}
diameter
\rho
and that the loss function
F
is such that
1
,
2
, and
3
hold (see
definition

3
). Furthermore, assume that
H_{D}(\cdot,v)
is
\beta
-smooth for all
\mathbf{v}\in S^{n}
with condition number
\kappa=\beta/\mu
. Run
algorithm

6
with
\mathcal{M}=
Minimax-AIPP.
Suppose
F
is of ERM form.

a) Let
\delta=0
and
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\min\{\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\}
yields

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}G_{D}(w_{\mathcal{A}})-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{D}(w)\leq 26\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}

in

T=\widetilde{O}\left(n^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{\beta\beta_{v}}{L^{2}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}}}\rho\right)
gradient evaluations and runtime

\widetilde{O}\left(n^{5/2}d\sqrt{\frac{\beta\beta_{v}}{L^{2}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}}}\rho\right).

b) Let
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}
gives

~\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}G_{D}(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta})-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{D}(w)\leq 13.5\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}

in

T=\widetilde{O}\left(n^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{\beta\beta_{v}}{L^{2}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}}}\rho\right)
gradient evaluations and runtime

\widetilde{O}\left(n^{5/2}d\sqrt{\frac{\beta\beta_{v}}{L^{2}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}}}\rho\right).

If, in addition,
H_{D}(w,\cdot)
is
\mu_{v}
-strongly concave in
v
for all
w\in B(0,R),
then the above bounds are all attained and the gradient complexity improves to
T=\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{\kappa\kappa_{v}}\right)
and runtime improves to
\widetilde{O}\left(nd\sqrt{\kappa\kappa_{v}}\right).

Notice that the
L_{2}
diameter of the cartesian product
S^{n}
scales as
\sqrt{n}\rho
: this explains the presence of the extra
\sqrt{n}
factor in the iteration complexity and runtime bounds (for non-strongly concave
H_{D}(w,\cdot)
) in
corollary

5.1
(since the adversary chooses
n
perturbations
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in S
) as compared to
proposition

5.2
. Also, note that if
F(\cdot,X,y)
is merely (non-strongly) convex,
L
-Lipschitz, and
\beta
-smooth, then applying the regularization technique (see
section

2.4
) can be used to derive similar results (with adversarial risk bounds resembling those in
theorem

C.2
). We omit the details here.

In the next and final subsection, we show how the above results for ERM
F
can be used to yield excess adversarial population loss bounds via the results in
section

4
.

5.2.4
From Adversarial Empirical Risk to Adversarial Population Loss

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution on
\cal{X}\times\cal{Y}
and
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\mathcal{X}+S)\times\cal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
be a loss function. In this subsection, we aim to understand how well
algorithm

6
(run on the empirical loss with
n
training samples
D=((x_{1},y_{1},\cdots,x_{n},y_{n}))
drawn i.i.d from
\mathcal{D}
) performs in minimizing the expected excess adversarial
population
loss of
f
on unseen data drawn from
\mathcal{D}
. To clarify the presentation of our adversarial population loss results, we change some notation, similar to
section

4
.

Change of Notations.
Denote the
expected adversarial population loss
by

G_{\mathcal{D}}(w):=\mathbb{E}_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}}[\max_{v\in S}f(w,x+v,y)].

In other words, the adversarial population loss measures how well our model performs on unseen data against an adversary. To emphasize the distinction between this function and the
empirical
adversarial loss with respect to a data set
D=((x_{1},y_{1},\cdots,x_{n},y_{n})),
we denote the latter by

\widehat{G}_{D}(w)=\max_{v_{i}\in S^{n},i\in[n]}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i}+v_{i},y_{i}).

Using the the results and methods from the preceding subsection and
section

4
, we can obtain population adversarial loss bounds in comparable runtimes to those for empirical adversarial loss. Here we state bounds under the smoothness and strong convexity assumptions on
f(\cdot,x+v,y)
(so that
\widehat{G}_{D}\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
). Bounds for the other three function classes studied in this paper can also be easily obtained, as consequences of our results from
section

3
and
section

4
. To begin, we state the excess adversarial population loss guarantee for
algorithm

5
, which is a direct consequence of
proposition

4.2
:

Proposition 5.3
.

Assume
f(\cdot,x+v,y)
is
\mu
-strongly convex,
L
-Lipschitz, and
\beta
-smooth (with condition number
\kappa=\beta/\mu
) for all
x+v\in\mathcal{X}+S
and
y\in\mathcal{Y}.
Also assume
f(w,x+\cdot,y)
is concave for all
w\in B(0,R),x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{Y}.
Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Let
D=(X,\mathbf{y})
be a data set of size
n
drawn i.i.d. according to
\mathcal{D}.
Run
algorithm

5
on the empirical loss
\widehat{G}_{D}(w)
to obtain
w_{\mathcal{A}}(D).

a) If
\delta=0
and
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1,
then

\mathbb{E}_{D\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}[G_{\mathcal{D}}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(D))]-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{\mathcal{D}}(w)\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{2}{n}+4\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right).

b) If
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1,
then

\mathbb{E}_{D\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}[G_{\mathcal{D}}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(D))]-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{\mathcal{D}}(w)\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{2}{n}+4\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right).

Note that the hypotheses in
proposition

5.3
imply that
\widehat{G}_{D}(w)
is
\mu
-strongly convex,
L
-Lipschitz, and
\beta
-smooth for all
D,
which is why the result follows immediately from
proposition

4.2
. As in
proposition

5.1
, the assumption that
f(w,x+\cdot,y)
is concave can be replaced with the weaker assumption that a saddle point of
\widehat{H}_{D}(w,\mathbf{v}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i}+v_{i},y_{i})
exists. If we assume additionally that
f(w,x+\cdot,y)
is
\beta_{v}
-smooth on
S
for all
w\in B(0,R)
and all
(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
(c.f.
3
), we obtain, via
corollary

5.1
, the following iteration complexity bounds:

Corollary 5.2
.

Assume
f(\cdot,x+v,y)
is
\mu
-strongly convex,
L
-Lipschitz, and
\beta
-smooth (with condition number
\kappa=\beta/\mu
) for all
x+v\in\mathcal{X}+S
and
y\in\mathcal{Y}.
Assume also that
f(w,x+\cdot,y)
is
\beta_{v}
-smooth and concave for all
w\in B(0,R),x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{Y}
(so that
\widehat{G}_{D}
is also
\beta_{v}
-smooth). Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Let
D=(X,\mathbf{y})
be a data set of size
n
drawn i.i.d. according to
\mathcal{D}.
Run
algorithm

6
on
\widehat{G}_{D}
with
\mathcal{M}=
Minimax-AIPP to obtain
w_{\mathcal{A}}(D).

a) Let
\delta=0
and
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}
yields

\mathbb{E}_{D\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}[G_{\mathcal{D}}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(D))]-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{\mathcal{D}}(w)\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{5}{n}+26\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right)

in
T=\widetilde{O}\left(n^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{\beta\beta_{v}}{L^{2}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}}}\rho\right)
gradient evaluations and runtime

\widetilde{O}\left(n^{5/2}d\sqrt{\frac{\beta\beta_{v}}{L^{2}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}}}\rho\right).

b) Let
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Setting
\alpha=\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}
yields

\mathbb{E}_{D\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}[G_{\mathcal{D}}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(D))]-\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}G_{\mathcal{D}}(w)\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{5}{n}+13.5\kappa\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right)

in
T=\widetilde{O}\left(n^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{\beta\beta_{v}}{L^{2}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}}}\rho\right)
gradient evaluations

and runtime

\widetilde{O}\left(n^{5/2}d\sqrt{\frac{\beta\beta_{v}}{L^{2}\min\left\{\kappa\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2},1\right\}}}\rho\right).

If, in addition,
f(w,x+\cdot,y)
is
\mu_{v}
-strongly concave for all
w\in B(0,R)
and
(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\cal{Y},
then the above adversarial loss bounds are all attained and the gradient complexity improves to
T=\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{\kappa\kappa_{v}}\right)
and runtime improves to
\widetilde{O}\left(nd\sqrt{\kappa\kappa_{v}}\right).

Implementation details not given for the output perturbation/localization step (first step) of the exponential + localization algorithm of
[
BST14
]
. However, the second step of their method alone has runtime of
O(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\frac{nd^{9}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\max\{1,\frac{L}{\mu}\})
and thus total runtime for their method, if localization were implemented using the methods of this paper, would exceed this quantity by an additive term comparable to our total runtime for that class. If

is in isotropic position, then runtime of the exponential mechanism improves by a factor of
O(d^{3}).

Objective perturbation bounds of
[
CMS11
]
are proved only for linear classifiers, but seem to be extendable to ERM functions satisfying the rank-one hessian assumption.

and

,

]

corollary

,
