Table 6:
ERM,
\delta>0
. ERM function class:
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
=
L
-Lipschitz,
\mu
-strongly convex,
\beta
-smooth. All excess risk bounds should be read as
\min\{LR,...\}
by taking the trivial algorithm. The assumptions
\frac{\sqrt{d}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1
or
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1
are needed to ensure the respective excess risk bounds shown are non-trivial. All excess risk bounds shown ignore the
\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)
or
c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}
factor which is present in all of them. Here
c_{\delta}=\sqrt{\log(\frac{2}{\sqrt{16\delta+1}-1})}
. The
\widetilde{O}(...)
notation is used here for runtime when there the runtime bound involves another logarithmic factor such as
\log(n)
or
\log(d)
. Our algorithm is the only one that has no restrictions on
\varepsilon>0.

Noisy SGD
[
BST14
]
doesn’t require smoothness, but excess risk bounds do not improve by adding smoothness.

[
WYX17
, Remark 4.1]
notes that the constraint
\varepsilon=\widetilde{O}(\frac{\kappa}{n^{2}})
can be relaxed at the cost of an additional logarithmic factor in their excess risk bound, by amplifying the variance of their noise. However, their algorithm can never ensure
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differential privacy for arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
due to their suboptimal choice of the Gaussian noise (c.f.
[
ZWB+19
]
).

Appendix B
Proofs of Results in
section

2

We recall the following basic fact about
\beta
-smooth convex functions, which will be used frequently in many proofs:

Lemma B.1
.

(Descent Lemma)
Let
f
be a convex
\beta
-smooth function on some domain
\mathcal{W}
. Then for any
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W},

f(w)-f(w^{\prime})\leq\langle\nabla f(w^{\prime}),w-w^{\prime}\rangle+\frac{\beta}{2}\|w-w^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}.

B.1
Proofs of Results in
section

2.2

B.1.1
Proof of
proposition

2.1

Recall the well-known post-processing property of differential privacy, which states that any function (e.g. projecting onto
B(0,R)
) of an
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private method is itself
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private
[
DR14
, Proposition 2.1]
. By this fact, it suffices to show that the algorithm
\mathcal{A}(X)=w^{*}(X)+z
(without projection) is differentially private. Assume first
\delta=0
. Then by the definition of differential privacy, it suffices to show that for any
s\in\mbox{range}(w_{\mathcal{A}})
(for which
\widehat{p}^{\prime}(s)\neq 0
) and any
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
such that
|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2,

\frac{\widehat{p}(s)}{\widehat{p}^{\prime}(s)}\leq e^{\varepsilon},

where
\widehat{p}
and
\widehat{p}^{\prime}
are the probability density functions (pdfs) of
\mathcal{A}(X)
and
\mathcal{A}(X^{\prime})
respectively. Now note that
\widehat{p}(s)=p_{z}(s-w^{*}(X))
and
\widehat{p}^{\prime}(s)=p_{z}(s-w^{*}(X^{\prime})),
where
p_{z}
is the pdf of the noise, given above. Then

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{p}(s)}{\widehat{p}^{\prime}(s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{z}(s-w^{*}(X))}{p_{z}(s-w^{*}(X^{\prime}))}=\exp\left({\frac{-\varepsilon\|s-w^{*}(X)\|_{2}+\varepsilon\|s-w^{*}(X^{\prime})\|_{2}}{\Delta_{F}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left({\frac{\varepsilon\|w^{*}(X)-w^{*}(X^{\prime})\|_{2}}{\Delta_{F}}}\right)\leq\exp({\varepsilon}),

where the second to last line uses the reverse triangle inequality and the last line uses the definition of
\Delta_{F}
. This establishes that
\mathcal{A}
is
(\varepsilon,0)
-differentially private. Now, by the post-processing property
[
DR14
]
, we conclude that the algorithm
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}(X)=w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X)=\Pi_{B(0,R)}(\mathcal{A}(X))
is
(\varepsilon,0)
- differentially private.

For
\delta>0
, the proof follows directly from the following result of
[
ZWB+19
]
:

Theorem B.1
.

(Theorem 5 in
[
ZWB+19
]
)
For
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),

(\varepsilon,\delta)
- differentially privacy can be achieved by adding Gaussian noise with mean
0
and standard deviation
\sigma=\frac{(c+\sqrt{c^{2}+\varepsilon})\Delta}{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}
to each dimension of a query with
L_{2}
sensitivity
\Delta.
Here
c=\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{16\delta+1}-1}\right)}.

Here, our “query" is
w^{*}(X),
which has sensitivity
\Delta_{F},
and hence
\mathcal{A}
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
. Applying the post-processing property again shows that
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private.

B.2
Proofs of Results in
section

2.3

B.2.1
Proof of
proposition

2.2

The proof relies on the following generalization (to non-differentiable functions on a possibly constrained (
\mathcal{W}\neq\mathbb{R}^{d}
) domain) of Lemma 7 from
[
CMS11
]
.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
G(w),g(w)
be functions on some convex closed set
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Suppose that
G(w)
is
\mu
-strongly convex and
G(w)+g(w)
is convex. Assume further that
g
is
L_{g}
-Lipschitz. Define
w_{1}=\arg\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}G(w),w_{2}=\arg\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}[G(w)+g(w)]
. Then
\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2}\leq\frac{L_{g}}{\mu}.

Proof of
lemma

B.2
.

At a point
w\in\mathcal{W},
let
h(w)
and
H(w)
output any subgradients of
g
and
G
, respectively. By first-order optimality conditions, for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, we have

\langle H(w_{1}),w-w_{1}\rangle\geq 0

and

\langle H(w_{2}),w-w_{2}\rangle+\langle h(w_{2}),w-w_{2}\rangle\geq 0.

Plugging
w_{2}
for
w
in the first inequality and
w_{1}
for
w
in the second inequality, and then subtracting inequalities gives:

\langle H(w_{1})-H(w_{2}),w_{1}-w_{2}\rangle\leq\langle h(w_{2}),w_{1}-w_{2}\rangle.

(11)

Now, by strong convexity of
G
, we have

\mu\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\langle H(w_{1})-H(w_{2}),w_{1}-w_{2}\rangle.

Combining this with
eq.

11
and using Cauchy-Schwartz yields:

\mu\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\langle H(w_{1})-H(w_{2}),w_{1}-w_{2}\leq\langle h(w_{2}),w_{1}-w_{2}\rangle\leq\|h(w_{2})\|_{2}\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2}.

Finally, using
L_{g}
-Lipschitzness of
g
and dividing the above inequality by
\mu\|w_{1}-w_{2}\|_{2}
gives the result.
∎

Now we can prove
proposition

2.2
.

Proof of
proposition

2.2
.

Let
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
such that (WLOG)
x_{n}\neq x^{\prime}_{n}
, but all other data points are the same.
Apply
lemma

B.2
to
G(w)=F(w,X),g(w)=F(w,X^{\prime})-F(w,X)
and note that
g
is
2L
-Lipschitz by the triangle inequality. For
F
of ERM form, we have
g(w)=\frac{1}{n}[f(w,x^{\prime}_{n})-f(w,x_{n})]
, which is
2\frac{L}{n}
-Lipschitz.
∎

B.2.2
Proof of
proposition

2.3

We begin with a simple lemma.

Lemma B.3
.

1. Let
z
be a random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with density
p_{z}(t)\propto\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon\|t\|_{2}}{\Delta}).

Then
\mathbb{E}\|z\|^{2}=\frac{d(d+1)\Delta^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

2. Let
b\sim N(0,\mathbf{I}_{d}\sigma^{2})
(Gaussian random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
). Then
~\mathbb{E}\|b\|_{2}^{2}=d\sigma^{2}.

Proof.

1.

Observe that
\|z\|_{2}\sim\Gamma(d,\frac{\Delta}{\varepsilon})
, which has mean
\mathbb{E}\|z\|_{2}=d\frac{\Delta}{\varepsilon}
and variance
\mathbb{E}\|z\|_{2}^{2}-(\mathbb{E}\|z\|_{2})^{2}=d(\frac{\Delta}{\varepsilon})^{2}
. Now solve for
\mathbb{E}\|z\|_{2}^{2}
.

2.

Next,
b
consists of
d
independent Gaussian random variables (i.i.d)
b_{i}\sim N(0,\sigma^{2}).
Hence
\mathbb{E}[\|b\|_{2}^{2}]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}b_{i}^{2}=d\sigma^{2}.

∎

We also recall the following basic fact about projections onto closed, convex sets:

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be a closed, convex set in
\mathbb{R}^{d},
and let
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Then
\pi=\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}(a)
if and only if
\langle a-\pi,w-\pi\rangle\leq 0
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}.

Proof.

Fix any
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
By definition,
\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}(a)=\arg\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\frac{1}{2}\|w-a\|_{2}^{2}:=\arg\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}h(w).
Then
\nabla h(w)=w-a
and by first-order optimality conditions,
\pi=\arg\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}h(w)
if and only if for all
w\in\mathcal{W},

\langle\nabla h(\pi),w-\pi\rangle\geq 0\Leftrightarrow\langle\pi-a,w-\pi\rangle\geq 0\Leftrightarrow\langle a-\pi,w-\pi\rangle\leq 0.

∎

Proof of
proposition

2.3
.

By
L
-Lipschitzness of
F,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq L\mathbb{E}\ |\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}(X)+z)-w^{*}(X)\|_{2}
.
Applying
lemma

B.4
to
a=w^{*}(X)+z
and
\pi=\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}(X)+z)
and using Cauchy-Schwartz gives

\displaystyle\|w^{*}(X)-\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}(X)+z)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\langle\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}(X)+z)-w^{*}(X),z\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}(X)+z)-w^{*}(X)\|_{2}\|z\|_{2}.

Dividing both sides of this inequality by
\|w^{*}(X)-\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}(X)+z)\|
shows that
|\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w^{*}(X)+z)-w^{*}(X)\|_{2}\leq\|z\|_{2}
for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Hence
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq L\mathbb{E}\|z\|_{2}.
Since
\|z\|_{2}\sim\Gamma(d,\frac{\Delta_{F}}{\varepsilon})
has mean
\mathbb{E}\|z\|_{2}=\frac{d\Delta_{F}}{\varepsilon},
part a) follows. For part b), combine the second part of
lemma

B.3
with Jensen’s inequality. This completes the proof.
∎

B.3
Proofs of Results in
section

2.4

B.3.1
Proof of
proposition

2.7

We will prove part 2 (ERM) here; the proof of part 1 is almost identical, but delete the
n
term everywhere it appears to account for the different sensitivity estimate in
proposition

2.2
.
To begin, decompose excess risk into 3 terms and then bound each one individually:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}_{\lambda}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{A^{\prime}_{\lambda}}[F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),X)-F_{\lambda}(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{i}}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{A^{\prime}_{\lambda}}F_{\lambda}(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)}_{\textcircled{\small{ii}}}+\underbrace{F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)}_{\textcircled{\small{iii}}}.

Suppose first
\delta=0
. Then we bound the terms as follows: \small{i}⃝ =
-\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathbb{E}\|w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X)\|_{2}^{2}\leq 0
; \small{ii}⃝
\leq\frac{(L+\lambda R)\Delta_{\lambda}d}{\varepsilon}
by
proposition

2.3
; and \small{iii}⃝
=[F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}(X),X)]+[F_{\lambda}(w^{*}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)]<0+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|w^{*}(X)\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}
since
w^{*}_{\lambda}(X)
is the unique minimizer of
F_{\lambda}(\cdot,X)
by strong convexity.
By
proposition

2.2
, we have
\Delta_{\lambda}\leq\frac{2(L+\lambda R)}{\lambda}
for
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}
and
\Delta_{\lambda}\leq\frac{2(L+\lambda R)}{\lambda n}
for
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}
.
For
\delta=0
, combining the above gives us:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}+\frac{2(L+\lambda R)^{2}}{\lambda}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)

for
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM},
and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}+\frac{2(L+\lambda R)^{2}}{\lambda}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)

for
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}
. Minimizing these expressions over
\lambda>0
yields (up to constant factors)
\lambda^{*}=\frac{L}{R\sqrt{1+\frac{\varepsilon n}{d}}}
for
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}
and
\lambda^{*}=\frac{L}{R\sqrt{1+\frac{\varepsilon}{d}}}
for
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}
. Plugging in these choices of
\lambda^{*}
and using elementary algebraic inequalities gives the results for
\delta=0
.

For
\delta>0
, we instead bound the three terms in the above decomposition as follows:
\small{i}⃝
\leq 0
, as before; \small{ii}⃝
\leq(L+\lambda R)\sqrt{d}\sigma_{\lambda}=(L+\lambda R)\sqrt{d}\left(\frac{c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{2}\varepsilon}\right)\Delta_{\lambda}
;
and \small{iii}⃝
\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}
, as before. Using
proposition

2.2
, we get

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}+\frac{2(L+\lambda R)^{2}}{\lambda}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)

for the ERM case; delete the
n
for non-ERM. Minimizing over
\lambda>0
yields (up to constants)
\lambda^{*}=\frac{L}{R\sqrt{1+\frac{\varepsilon n}{d^{1/2}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}}}
for ERM
F
. Plugging this in and using elementary algebraic inequalities (e.g. note
L+\lambda^{*}R\leq 2L
) and the assumptions in the statement of the proposition finishes the proof. Delete the
n
everywhere and follow line for line for non-ERM.

B.3.2
Proof of
proposition

2.4

By the descent lemma,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}[F(w^{*}(X)+z),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}\langle\nabla_{w}F(w^{*}(X),X),w^{*}(X)+z-w^{*}(X)\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{\beta}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}\|w^{*}(X)+z-w^{*}(X)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}\|z\|_{2}^{2}.

Now recall (
lemma

B.3
) that for
\delta=0
,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}\|z\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{d(d+1)\Delta_{F}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
, whereas for
\delta>0
,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}\|z_{\delta}\|_{2}^{2}=d\sigma^{2}=\frac{d}{2}\left(\frac{c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\Delta_{F}^{2}.

Combining the above gives the result.

B.3.3
Proof of
proposition

2.5

The proof is an extension of the argument used in
[
BST14
]
. Define the following unscaled hard instance:

f(w,x):=\frac{1}{2}\|Mw-x\|_{2}^{2},

for
w,x\in B(0,1)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d},
where
M
is a symmetric positive definite
d\times d
matrix satisfying
\frac{\mu}{\beta}\mathbf{I}\preccurlyeq M^{2}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{I}.
Note that this implies
\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\beta}}\mathbf{I}\preccurlyeq M\preccurlyeq\mathbf{I}.
Then for any
w,x\in B(0,1)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\nabla f(w,x)=M^{T}(Mw-x)\implies\|\nabla f(w,x)\|_{2}\leq\|M^{2}\|_{2}\|w\|_{2}+\|M^{T}\|_{2}\|x\|_{2}\leq 2,

so
f(\cdot,x)
is 2-Lipschitz,
and

\frac{\mu}{\beta}\mathbf{I}\preccurlyeq\nabla^{2}f(w,x)=M^{2}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{I},

so
f
is
\frac{\mu}{\beta}
-strongly convex and
1
-smooth.
Hence
F(w,X):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(w,x_{i})\in\mathcal{H}^{ERM}_{1,\frac{\mu}{\beta},2,1}.

We will require the following lemma from
[
BST14
]
:

Lemma B.5
.

[
BST14
, Lemma 5.1]

1.

Let
n,d\in\mathbb{N},\varepsilon>0.
For every
\varepsilon
-differentially private algorithm
\mathcal{A},
there is a data set
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in B(0,1)^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
such that with probability at least
1/2
(over the randomness in
\mathcal{A}
), we have

\|\mathcal{A}(X)-\bar{X}\|_{2}=\Omega\left(\min\left\{1,\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right\}\right),

where
\bar{X}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}.

2.

Let
n,d\in\mathbb{N},\varepsilon>0,\delta=o(1/n).
For every
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private algorithm
\mathcal{A},
there is a data set
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in B(0,1)^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
such that with probability at least
1/3
(over the randomness in
\mathcal{A}
), we have

\|\mathcal{A}(X)-\bar{X}\|_{2}=\Omega\left(\min\left\{1,\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}\right\}\right),

where
\bar{X}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}.

Note that for any
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private algorithm
\mathcal{A},
the algorithm
M^{-1}\mathcal{A}
is also
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private by post-processing
[
DR14
]
. Moreover, since
M
is a bijection, every
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private algorithm
\mathcal{A}
can be written as
M\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
for
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private algorithm
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}.
Thus, we can replace
\|\mathcal{A}(X)-\bar{X}\|_{2}
by
\|M\mathcal{A}(X)-\bar{X}\|_{2}
in the statement of
lemma

B.5
.

Now observe that for any
w
and
X=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}),

\nabla F(w,X)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M^{T}(Mw-x_{i})\implies w^{*}(X)=(M^{T}M)^{-1}M^{T}\bar{X}=M^{-1}\bar{X},

and

\displaystyle F(w,X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}\left[n\|Mw\|_{2}^{2}-2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\langle Mw,x_{i}\rangle-\langle\bar{X},x_{i}\rangle\right)-n\|\bar{X}\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\|Mw\|_{2}^{2}-2\langle Mw,\bar{X}\rangle+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|Mw-\bar{X}\|_{2}^{2}.

Combining this with
lemma

B.5
and the discussion that followed, and applying Markov’s inequality implies that for any
\varepsilon
-differentially private algorithm
\mathcal{A},
there exists a data set
X\in B(0,1)^{n}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)=\Omega\left(\min\left\{1,\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right\}\right).

(12)

Likewise, for any
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private algorithm
\mathcal{A},
there exists a data set
X\in B(0,1)^{n}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)=\Omega\left(\min\left\{1,\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\right\}\right).

(13)

It remains to properly scale
F.
To this end, define
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{X}=B(0,R)
and
\widetilde{f}:B(0,R)\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{x}):=\frac{\beta}{2}\|M\widetilde{w}-\widetilde{x}\|_{2}^{2},
where
\widetilde{w}=Rw
and
\widetilde{x}=Rx.
Then
\widetilde{F}(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{X}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{x_{i}})\in\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
with
L=2\beta R
and

\widetilde{F}(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{X})-\widetilde{F}(\widetilde{w}^{*}(\widetilde{X}),\widetilde{X})=\frac{\beta}{2}\|M\widetilde{w}-\bar{\widetilde{X}}\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{\beta R^{2}}{2}\|Mw-\bar{X}\|_{2}^{2},

where
\widetilde{w}^{*}(\widetilde{X})=\argmin_{\widetilde{w}\in B(0,R)}\widetilde{F}(\widetilde{w},\widetilde{X})
and
\bar{\widetilde{X}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{x_{i}}.
Therefore, applying the unscaled lower bounds above from
eq.

12
and
eq.

13
completes the proof.

B.3.4
Proof of
proposition

2.8

As in the proof of
proposition

2.7
, we decompose excess risk into three terms and then bound each one individually:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{A}[F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),X)-F_{\lambda}(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{a}}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{A}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)}_{\textcircled{\small{b}}}+\underbrace{F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)}_{\textcircled{\small{c}}}.

We prove part 2 (ERM) first. Assume first that
\delta=0
. Then we bound the terms as follows: \small{a}⃝ =
-\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathbb{E}\|w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X)\|_{2}^{2}\leq 0
; \small{b}⃝
\leq(\beta+\lambda)(2\frac{L+\lambda R}{\lambda})^{2}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}
by
(\beta+\lambda)
-smoothness and
(L+\lambda R)
-Lipschitzness of
F_{\lambda}
, and
proposition

2.4
combined with
proposition

2.2
; and \small{c}⃝
=[F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}(X),X)]+[F_{\lambda}(w^{*}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)]<0+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|w^{*}(X)\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}
since
w^{*}_{\lambda}(X)
is the unique minimizer of
F_{\lambda}(\cdot,X)
by strong convexity. Putting these estimates together, setting
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}
, noting
\lambda R\leq L
by the parameter assumptions and hence
\lambda\leq 2\beta
(by the inequality
L\leq 2\beta R
which holds for all
F\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}+4(\beta+\lambda)\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\frac{(L+\lambda R)^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}+\frac{4(3\beta)(2L)^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 48.5\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.

Now suppose
\delta>0
. Then the \small{b}⃝ term in the decomposition is bounded as

\textcircled{\small{b}}\leq 4(\beta+\lambda)\left(\frac{L+\lambda R}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}

by
(\beta+\lambda)
-smoothness and
(L+\lambda R)
-Lipschitzness of
F_{\lambda}
, and
proposition

2.4
combined with
proposition

2.2
. Then the other two terms are bounded as above for
\delta=0
. Set
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}
.
Again note that
\lambda R\leq L
and
\lambda\leq 2\beta
by the parameter assumptions and the inequality
L\leq 2\beta R
for
F\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}
. Then, by similar steps as above, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}+4\left(\beta+\lambda\right)\left(\frac{L+\lambda R}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}+48\frac{\beta L^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 48.5\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.

To prove part 1, set
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}
when
\delta=0
and
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}
for
\delta>0
. Then the proof of part 1 follows line for line as the proof of part 2, except with “
n
" deleted everywhere.

Appendix C
Proofs for
section

3
and Additional Results for Non-Strongly-Convex

C.1
Proofs of Results in
section

3.1

C.1.1
Proof of
proposition

3.1

Define
\Delta_{T}:=\sup_{|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2}\|w_{T}(X)-w_{T}(X^{\prime})\|_{2}.
By standard arguments (see proof of
proposition

2.1
) and our choice of noise, it suffices to show that
\Delta_{T}\leq\Delta_{F}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}
.
Now, since
F(w_{T}(X),X)-F(w^{*},X)\leq\alpha
by the choice of
T=T(\alpha)
, and since
F(\cdot,X)
is
\mu
-strongly convex, we have

\|w_{T}-w^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{\mu}[F(w_{T},X)-F(w^{*},X)-\langle\nabla F(w^{*},X),w_{T}-w^{*}\rangle]\leq\frac{2}{\mu}\alpha.

Hence for any data sets
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
such that
|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\|w_{T}(X)-w_{T}(X^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|w_{T}(X)-w^{*}(X)\|+\|w^{*}(X)-w^{*}(X^{\prime})\|+\|w^{*}(X^{\prime})-w_{T}(X^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\mu}\alpha}+\Delta_{F}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\mu}\alpha}.

C.1.2
Proof of
theorem

3.1

Notice

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)=\mathbb{E}F(\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w_{T}(X)+\widehat{z}),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[F(\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w_{T}(X)+\widehat{z}),X)-F(w_{T}(X),X)]+\mathbb{E}[F(w_{T}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)]

\displaystyle\leq L\mathbb{E}\|\widehat{z}\|_{2}+\alpha,

where we used
lemma

B.4
and the non-expansiveness property of projection in the last inequality.
Now for
\delta=0,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\sqrt{2}L\left(\Delta_{F}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}\right)\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)+\alpha
by
lemma

B.3
. Then using
proposition

2.2
to bound
\Delta_{F}
proves the first statement in each of 1a) and 2a). Similarly, for
\delta>0
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq L\left(\Delta_{F}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}\right)\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)+\alpha

by
lemma

B.3
. Appealing again to
proposition

2.2
completes the proof. The verification that the prescribed choices of
\alpha
achieve the respective conceptual upper bounds is routine, using the assumptions stated in the theorem.

C.1.3
Proof of of
corollary

3.1

In line 4 of
algorithm

2
, note that
\mathbb{E}_{i\sim unif[n]}\|g(w,x_{i})\|_{2}^{2}\leq L^{2}
since
f(\cdot,x)
is
L
-Lipschitz for all
x\in\mathcal{X}.
Also,
\mathbb{E}_{i\sim unif[n]}[g(w,x_{i})]=G(w,x_{i})\in\partial F(w,X).
Therefore, the result follows from
[
Bub15
, Theorem 6.2]
, which says that
F(\widehat{w_{T}},X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu(T+1)},
where
\widehat{w_{T}}
is the weighted average of the first
T
iterates
w_{t},t\in[T]
returned by
algorithm

2
.

C.1.4
Proof of
theorem

3.2

Write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}F(w_{T}(X)+\widehat{z},X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}F(w_{T}(X)+\widehat{z},X)-F(w_{T}(X),X)+F(w_{T}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\langle\nabla F(w_{T}(X),X),\widehat{z}\rangle+\mathbb{E}\|\widehat{z}\|_{2}^{2}+\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{2}\mathbb{E}\|\widehat{z}\|_{2}^{2}+\alpha,

where we used the descent lemma to get the inequality. Now for
\delta=0
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\beta\left(\Delta_{F}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+\alpha.

by
lemma

B.3
.
Substituting the bounds on
\Delta_{F}
from
proposition

2.2
proves the first statement in each of 1a) and 2a).
Similarly, for
\delta>0
,

~\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\left[\frac{d\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\left(\Delta_{F}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right]+\alpha.

Again appealing to
proposition

2.2
establishes the first statements in 1b) and 2b). Verification that the prescribed choices of
\alpha
achieve the claimed bounds is straightforward.

C.2
Efficient Implementation for Convex, Lipschitz functions

In this subsection, we proceed similarly as in
section

2.4
, where we used regularization to obtain a strongly convex objective
F_{\lambda}(w,X)=F(w,X)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|w\|_{2}^{2}
, optimized
F_{\lambda}
using our techniques for strongly convex functions, and then add noise (calibrated to the sensitivity of
F_{\lambda}
) to ensure privacy. The key difference here is that, as in the previous subsection, we do not obtain
w^{*}_{\lambda}(X)
exactly, but rather get an approximate minimizer. Our Black Box implementation procedure is described in
algorithm

7
. Our first result is that this method is differentially private.

Algorithm 7

Black Box Output Perturbation Algorithm with Regularization for
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}

1:

Number of data points
n\in\mathbb{N},
dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
of data, non-private (possibly randomized) optimization method
\mathcal{M}
, privacy parameters
\varepsilon>0,\delta\geq 0
, data universe
\mathcal{X},
data set
X\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, function
F(w,X)\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}
, accuracy and regularization parameters
\alpha>0,\lambda>0
with corresponding iteration number
T=T(\alpha,\lambda)\in\mathbb{N}
(such that
\mathbb{E}[F_{\lambda}(w_{T}(X),X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}(X),X)]\leq\alpha
).

2:

Run
\mathcal{M}
on
F_{\lambda}(w,X)=F(w,X)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|w\|_{2}^{2}
for
T=T(\alpha)
iterations to ensure
\mathbb{E}F_{\lambda}(w_{T}(X),X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda},X)\leq\alpha
.

3:

Add noise to ensure privacy:
w_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}:=w_{T}+\hat{z_{\lambda}}
, where the density
p(\hat{z_{\lambda}})
of
\hat{z_{\lambda}}
is proportional to

\begin{cases}\exp\left\{-\frac{\varepsilon\|\hat{z_{\lambda}}\|_{2}}{\Delta_{\lambda}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}}}\right\}&\mbox{if }\delta=0\\
\exp\left\{-\frac{2\varepsilon^{2}\|\hat{z_{\lambda}}\|_{2}^{2}}{(\Delta_{\lambda}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}})^{2}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})^{2}}\right\}&\mbox{if }\delta>0,\end{cases}

where
\Delta_{\lambda}:=\frac{2(L+\lambda R)}{\lambda n}

is an upper bound on the
L_{2}
sensitivity of
F_{\lambda}.

4:

return

w_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}.

Proposition C.1
.

algorithm

7
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private. Moreover,
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}(X):=\Pi_{B(0,R)}\left(w_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}(X)\right)
is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private, where
w_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}(X)}
denotes the output of
algorithm

7
.

Next, we establish excess risk bounds for the generic
algorithm

7
that depend on
\alpha,
and show that the conceptual excess risk bounds from
section

2.4
can be obtained by choosing
\alpha
appropriately.

Theorem C.1
.

Suppose
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}
. Run
algorithm

7
on
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}
with
\lambda>0
chosen as follows: If
\delta=0,
set
\lambda=\frac{L}{R\sqrt{1+\frac{\varepsilon n}{d}}}
.
If
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right),
set
\lambda=\frac{L}{R\sqrt{1+\frac{\varepsilon n}{d^{1/2}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})}}}.

Denote
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}(X)=w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}(X):=\Pi_{B(0,R)}\left(w_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}(X)\right),
where
w_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}(X)
is the output of
algorithm

7
.

a) Let
\delta=0,\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.

Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 16.5LR\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}}+32\sqrt{\alpha}\sqrt{LR}(\frac{\varepsilon n}{d})^{1/4}(1+\frac{d}{\varepsilon}).

In particular, setting
\alpha=\frac{LR\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{3/2}}{(1+\frac{d}{\varepsilon})^{2}}
implies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 49LR\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}}.

b) Let
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1
.
Then

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 13LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+12\sqrt{\alpha}\sqrt{LR}\left(\frac{\varepsilon n}{\sqrt{d}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})}\right)^{1/4}.

In particular, setting
\alpha=LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{3/2}
implies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 25LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}.

Instantiating
algorithm

7
with fast stochastic optimization algorithms allow us to achieve the above excess risk bounds efficiently. Note that
F_{\lambda}(\cdot,X)
is
(L+\lambda R)
-Lipschitz and
\lambda
-strongly convex. Recall that in this case,
T=\left\lceil\frac{2(L+\lambda R)^{2}}{\lambda\alpha}\right\rceil
iterations of stochastic subgradient descent are sufficient for finding a point
{w_{T}}
such that
\mathbb{E}F_{\lambda}(w_{T},X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)\leq\alpha
. Plugging in the value of
\lambda
prescribed in
theorem

C.1
, we have the following:

Corollary C.1
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}.
Run
algorithm

7
with
\mathcal{M}
as the stochastic subgradient method and set
\lambda
as specified in
theorem

C.1
,
\alpha
and
T
as prescribed below. Denote
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}(X)=w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}(X):=\Pi_{B(0,R)}\left(w_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}(X)\right),
where
w_{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}(X)
is the output of
algorithm

7
.

a) Let
\delta=0
and
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then setting
\alpha=\frac{LR\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{3/2}}{(1+\frac{d}{\varepsilon})^{2}}
and
T=\left\lceil 12n^{2}(1+\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{d}\right)^{2})\right\rceil
implies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 49LR\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}}

in runtime
O\left(dn^{2}\max\left\{1,\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{d}\right)^{2}\right\}\right).

b) Let
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Then setting
\alpha=LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{3/2}
and
T=\left\lceil 12\frac{n^{2}\varepsilon}{dc_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}})\right\rceil
implies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 25LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}

in runtime
O(n^{2}\varepsilon).

There are two existing algorithms for
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}
that we are aware of that provide excess risk and runtime bounds. For
\delta=0,
the exponential mechanism of
[
BST14
]
has excess risk
O\left(LR\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)\right)
and runtime
\widetilde{O}\left(R^{2}d^{6}n^{3}\max\{d,\varepsilon nR\}\right),
which is clearly not very practical for large-scale problems. For
\delta>0,
the noisy SGD method of
[
BST14
]
has excess risk
O\left(LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\log(n)\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)\right)
and runtime
O(n^{2}d).
This method requires
\varepsilon\leq 2\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}
to be private, however, which restricts how small excess risk can get. Although both of these excess empirical risk bounds are tighter than the ones we provide via output perturbation in most parameter regimes, in
section

4
, we prove tighter
population loss
bounds (which is arguably more important than empirical risk) than the two competing algorithms of
[
BST14
]
noted above.

Next, we assume additionally that
F
is
\beta
-smooth.

C.2.1
Smooth, convex, Lipschitz functions.

When we add the smoothness assumption, we need to change the regularization parameter in
algorithm

7
in order to obtain tighter excess risk bounds.

Theorem C.2
.

F\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}.
Run
algorithm

7
with accuracy
\alpha
and set
\lambda
as follows: if
\delta=0
, set
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.
If
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, set
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.

a) Let
\delta=0
and
~\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{L}{R\beta}.
Then,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 48.5\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}+\alpha\left[3\left(\frac{R\beta}{L}\right)^{2/3}\frac{d^{4/3}n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}}+1\right]+12\sqrt{\alpha}R\beta^{1/2}\frac{d}{\varepsilon}.

In particular, setting
\alpha=\min\left\{\frac{L^{4/3}R^{2/3}}{\beta^{1/3}}(\frac{\varepsilon}{d})^{2/3}\frac{1}{n^{4/3}},\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right\}
implies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 65\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.

b) Let
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
~\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{L}{R\beta}.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)

\displaystyle\leq 48.5\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}

\displaystyle\;\;\;+\alpha\left[1+8n^{2/3}\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{2/3}\frac{(d(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})^{2})^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}}\right]+69\sqrt{\alpha}R\sqrt{\beta}\frac{\sqrt{d}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}.

In particular, setting
\alpha=\min\left\{\frac{L^{4/3}R^{2/3}}{\beta^{1/3}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon n}{\sqrt{d}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})}\right)^{2/3}\frac{1}{n^{2}},\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right\}
implies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 127\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.

For efficient private optimization, we can instantiate our Black Box Algorithm with an accelerated stochastic method, such Katyusha
[
AZ18
]
, on the regularized objective. Recall that Katyusha finds an
\alpha
-suboptimal point of
F_{\lambda}(\cdot,X)
in runtime
\widetilde{O}\left(d\left(n+\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\frac{\beta+\lambda}{\lambda}}\right)\right)
(where
\widetilde{O}
hides a log factor depending on
\frac{1}{\alpha}
) since
F_{\lambda}
is
(\beta+\lambda)
-smooth and
\lambda
-strongly convex. Putting in the choices of
\lambda
and
\alpha
prescribed by
theorem

C.2
gives the following:

Corollary C.2
.

For
F\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
, take
\mathcal{M}
to be
algorithm

3
(Katyusha) in
algorithm

7
and
\lambda
as prescribed in
theorem

C.2
,
\alpha
and
T
as given below.

a) If
\delta=0
and
\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{L}{R\beta}
then taking
\alpha=\frac{L^{4/3}R^{2/3}}{\beta^{1/3}}(\frac{\varepsilon}{d})^{2/3}\frac{1}{n^{4/3}}
and

T=O\left(n+n^{5/6}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{d}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}\log\left(\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}n^{4/3}\right)\right)

implies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 65\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}

in runtime

\widetilde{O}\left(nd+n^{5/6}d^{2/3}\varepsilon^{1/3}\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}\right).

b) If
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{L}{R\beta},
then taking
\alpha=\frac{L^{4/3}R^{2/3}}{\beta^{1/3}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon n}{\sqrt{d}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})}\right)^{2/3}\frac{1}{n^{2}},
and
~T=O\left(n+n^{5/6}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{d}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}\log\left(\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}n^{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right)\right)
implies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq 127\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}

in runtime

\widetilde{O}\left(nd+n^{5/6}d^{5/6}\varepsilon^{1/6}\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}\right).

Remark C.1
.

Since
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
, by
corollary

C.1
, we obtain the following upper bounds on the excess risk of functions in
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}:\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim\min\left\{LR,LR\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}},\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right\}
for
\delta=0
;
and
~\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\lesssim\min\left\{LR,LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2},\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right\}
for
\delta>0.

Now, to derive upper bounds on the runtimes for obtaining the second (middle) excess risk bounds in the above minima (i.e. the excess risk bounds for
\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}
) under the additional assumption of
\beta
-smoothness, we use the regularization term
\lambda
and the corresponding
\alpha
that are prescribed in
theorem

C.1
. For
\delta=0,
plugging these choices of
\lambda
and
\alpha
into the runtime expression for Katyusha gives the following runtime for achieving excess risk
O\left(LR\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}}\right)
:
\widetilde{O}\left(d\left(n+\sqrt{n\left(\frac{\beta+\lambda}{\lambda}\right)}\right)\right)=\widetilde{O}\left(d\left(n+n^{3/4}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{d}\right)^{1/4}\sqrt{\frac{\beta R}{L}}\right)\right)=\widetilde{O}\left(nd+n^{3/4}d^{3/4}\varepsilon^{1/4}\sqrt{\frac{\beta R}{L}}\right).
Likewise, for
\delta>0,
the runtime for obtaining excess risk
O\left(LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}\right)
by Katyusha is
\widetilde{O}\left(d\left(n+n^{3/4}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}}\right)^{1/4}\sqrt{\frac{\beta R}{L}}\right)\right)=\\
\widetilde{O}\left(nd+n^{3/4}\varepsilon^{1/8}d^{7/8}\sqrt{\frac{\beta R}{L}}\right).

There are several alternative efficient differentially private algorithms for smooth convex ERM that have been proposed previously, but
our method is faster and arguably simpler
than all of these. Here we discuss only the methods that have practical implementation schemes and runtime guarantees (see
section

2.3.1
and
section

2.4.1
for discussion of objective perturbation) and those that require smoothness (see
section

C.2
for discussion of exponential mechanism and noisy SGD), to avoid redundancy. The gradient descent-based output perturbation method of
[
ZZMW17
]
achieves excess risk of
O\left(\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right)
in runtime
O\left(n^{5/3}d^{1/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}\left(\frac{L}{R\beta}\right)^{2/3}\right)
when
\delta=0
. When
\delta>0
, they get excess empirical risk
\widetilde{O}\left(\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right)
in runtime
\widetilde{O}\left(n^{5/3}d^{2/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{2/3}\right),
but they require
\varepsilon\leq 1
in order to be differentially private (by their choice of Gaussian noise and
[
ZWB+19
]
). Thus, by
remark

C.1
, our excess risk bounds match or beat (when
\beta
or
R
is very large) theirs in every parameter regime (see Section 2.5 for details). In addition, our runtime is significantly faster by way of using Katyusha instead of gradient descent. Indeed, when our risk bounds match, we improve runtime by a factor of
O\left(n^{5/6}\frac{\beta R}{L}\frac{\varepsilon}{d}\right)^{1/3}
for
\delta=0
and
\widetilde{O}\left(n^{5/6}\frac{\beta R}{L}\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{d}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})}\right)^{1/3}
for
\delta>0,
while also permitting any
\varepsilon>0.

For
\delta>0,
the other notable method for
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
is noisy SVRG of
[
WYX17
]
. This method achieves nearly optimal excess empirical risk of
\widetilde{O}\left(LR\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}\right)
in runtime
\widetilde{O}\left(n\left(\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)\varepsilon\sqrt{d}+d\right)\right),
provided
\beta
is “sufficiently large”
[
WYX17
, Theorem 4.4]
. By contrast, our method works for arbitrarily small
\beta
and (ignoring log terms) is faster whenever
d^{2}\leq n\varepsilon^{5}\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{4}
due to acceleration.

In the next section, we use the ERM results from this Section (and Section 2) to derive strong differentially private guarantees on the excess
population loss
of Lipschitz convex (and strongly convex) loss functions. The population loss bounds are realized in as little runtime as was needed to obtain the ERM risk bounds in this Section, making them the fastest known runtimes for differentially private optimization when
\delta=0
and nearly the fastest for
\delta>0
.

C.3
Proofs of Results in
section

C.2

C.3.1
Proof of
proposition

C.1

By standard arguments, it suffices to show that
\sup_{|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2}\|w_{T}(X)-w_{T}(X^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq\Delta_{\lambda}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}.
Let
|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2
. Then

\displaystyle\|w_{T}(X)-w_{T}(X^{\prime})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|w_{T}(X)-w^{*}_{\lambda}(X)\|+\|w^{*}_{\lambda}(X)-w^{*}_{\lambda}(X^{\prime})\|+\|w^{*}_{\lambda}(X^{\prime})-w_{T}(X^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}}+\Delta_{\lambda}+\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}}.

The last line follows because:
\lambda
-strong convexity of
F_{\lambda}
implies the middle term is bounded according to
proposition

2.2
and the other two terms are both bounded by a similar argument as in
proposition

3.1
, again using
\lambda
-strong convexity of
F_{\lambda}
. Finally, the post-processing property of differential privacy
[
DR14
, Proposition 2.1]
implies that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is also differentially private.

C.3.2
Proof of
theorem

C.1

To begin, decompose excess risk into 3 terms:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)=\\
&\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}[F(\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w_{T}(X)+z_{\lambda}),X)-F_{\lambda}(\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w_{T}(X)+z_{\lambda}),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{a}}}\\
&+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F_{\lambda}(\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w_{T}(X)+z_{\lambda}),X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)}_{\textcircled{\small{b}}}+\underbrace{F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)}_{\textcircled{\small{c}}}.\end{split}

Now bound the terms as follows: \small{a}⃝ =
-\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathbb{E}\|w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X)\|_{2}^{2}\leq 0
;

\textcircled{\small{b}}\leq(L+\lambda R)\mathbb{E}\|\Pi_{B(0,R)}(w_{T}(X)+z_{\lambda})-w^{*}_{\lambda}(X)\|\leq(L+\lambda R)\left[\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}}+\mathbb{E}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}\right],

using contractivity of the projection map,
\lambda
-strong convexity of
F_{\lambda}
and our choice of
T=T(\alpha).
Also,

\displaystyle\textcircled{\small{c}}=[F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}(X),X)]+[F_{\lambda}(w^{*}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)]

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 0+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|w^{*}(X)\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}

since
w^{*}_{\lambda}(X)
is the unique minimizer of
F_{\lambda}(\cdot,X)
by strong convexity. Hence
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq(L+\lambda R)\left[\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}}+\mathbb{E}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}\right]+\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}
.

Assume that
F\in\mathcal{G}_{L,R}^{ERM}
: the proof of the non-ERM case is very similar (but simpler since
n
disappears).
First suppose
\delta=0
, so
\mathbb{E}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}d(\Delta_{\lambda}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}})}{\varepsilon}.
Then
\Delta_{\lambda}\leq\frac{2(L+\lambda R)}{\lambda n}\leq\frac{4L}{\lambda n}\leq 4\sqrt{2}R\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{nd}}
by our choice of
\lambda
and assumption that
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1
. Also,
\frac{1}{\lambda}<\sqrt{2}\frac{R}{L}\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}}.
Using these estimates and the above estimate for excess risk gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\\
2L\left[2\sqrt{\alpha}\sqrt{\frac{R}{L}}(\frac{\varepsilon n}{d})^{1/4}+\sqrt{2}\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\left(4\sqrt{2}R\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{nd}}+4\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\alpha}\sqrt{\frac{R}{L}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon n}{d}\right)^{1/4}\right)\right]+LR\frac{\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}}}{2}\\
\leq 16\sqrt{\alpha}\sqrt{LR}(\frac{\varepsilon n}{d})^{1/4}(1+\frac{d}{\varepsilon})+16.5LR\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}},

(14)

as claimed. Then plug in
\alpha.

Next, suppose
\delta\in(0,1/2).
Then
\mathbb{E}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon}\left(\Delta_{\lambda}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}}\right).
Now

\lambda=\frac{L}{R\sqrt{1+\frac{\varepsilon n}{d^{1/2}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})}}}<\frac{L}{R\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon n}{d^{1/2}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})}}}\leq\frac{L}{R}\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}}

and
~\Delta_{\lambda}\leq\frac{4L}{\lambda n}\leq 4\sqrt{2}\frac{R}{n}(\frac{\varepsilon n}{\sqrt{d}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})^{2}})^{1/2}.
Using these estimates and the estimates for each component of excess risk, as above, gives
the first statement of part 2b). Using the assumptions and estimates above, it is easy to verify that the prescribed choices of
\alpha
yield the second statements in each part of the theorem.

C.3.3
Proof of
theorem

C.2

We decompose excess risk into 3 terms:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{A}[F(w_{T}(X)+z_{\lambda},X)-F_{\lambda}(w_{T}(X)+z_{\lambda},X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{a}}}+\\
\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{A}F_{\lambda}(w_{T}(X)+z_{\lambda},X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)}_{\textcircled{\small{b}}}+\underbrace{F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)}_{\textcircled{\small{c}}}.

Now bound the terms as follows: \small{a}⃝ =
-\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathbb{E}\|w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X)\|_{2}^{2}\leq 0
. By the descent lemma (note
F_{\lambda}(\cdot,X)
is
(\beta+\lambda)
-smooth) and mean-zero noise
z_{\lambda}
, we have

\displaystyle\textcircled{\small{b}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[F_{\lambda}(w_{T}+z_{\lambda},X)-F_{\lambda}(w_{T},X)]+\mathbb{E}[F_{\lambda}(w_{T},X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda},X)]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\langle\nabla F_{\lambda}(w_{T}(X),X)),z_{\lambda}\rangle+\frac{\beta+\lambda}{2}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}^{2}]+\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta+\lambda}{2}\mathbb{E}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}^{2}+\alpha.

Lastly, \small{c}⃝
=[F_{\lambda}(w^{*}_{\lambda}(X),X)-F_{\lambda}(w^{*}(X),X)]+[F_{\lambda}(w^{*}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)]<0+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|w^{*}(X)\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}
since
w^{*}_{\lambda}(X)
is the unique minimizer of
F_{\lambda}(\cdot,X)
by strong convexity. Hence
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}+(\beta+\lambda)\mathbb{E}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}^{2}+\alpha.

Assume that
F\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}^{ERM}
: the proof of the non-ERM case is very similar, but simpler since
n
disappears.
First suppose
\delta=0
, so
\mathbb{E}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2d^{2}(2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}}+\Delta_{\lambda})^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}.
Note that for our choice of
\lambda
given in

theorem

C.2
,
~\lambda\leq\frac{L}{R}\leq 2\beta
and
(L+\lambda R)\leq 2L
by the assumption
~\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{L}{R\beta}
and since
~L\leq 2\beta R
always holds for
~F\in\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}.
Also,
~\Delta_{\lambda}\leq\frac{2(L+\lambda R)}{\lambda n}\leq\frac{4L}{\lambda n}\leq 4(\frac{LR^{2}}{\beta})^{1/3}(\frac{\varepsilon n}{d})^{2/3}
. Therefore,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}+3\beta\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\left[16\frac{L^{2}}{\lambda^{2}n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda}+4\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{\lambda}}\frac{L}{\lambda n}\right]+\alpha.

Plugging in
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}
, using the estimates above and the fact that
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1
by assumption gives

\lambda R^{2},~\beta\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\frac{L^{2}}{\lambda^{2}n^{2}}\leq\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}.

Also, by the choice of
\lambda,

\beta\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\left[\frac{\alpha}{\lambda}+4\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{\lambda}}\frac{L}{\lambda n}\right]\leq\alpha\left[\left(\frac{R\beta}{L}\right)^{2/3}\frac{d^{4/3}n^{2/3}}{\varepsilon^{4/3}}\right]+4\sqrt{\alpha}R\beta^{1/2}\frac{d}{\varepsilon}.

Putting these pieces together proves the first statement in part 2a). Verifying the second statement is routine, using the assumptions stated in the theorem and the estimates obtained above.

Now suppose
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. Then
\mathbb{E}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{d\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)\left(2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}}+\Delta_{\lambda}\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}.
Again, the assumption
\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{L}{R\beta}
implies
\lambda\leq 2\beta
and
L+\lambda R\leq 2L
. Thus,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}^{\delta}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(X),X)\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}+3\beta\frac{d\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left[\Delta_{\lambda}^{2}+4\sqrt{2}\Delta_{\lambda}\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{\lambda}}+8\frac{\alpha}{\lambda}\right]+\alpha\\
\leq 48.5\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}+3\beta\frac{d\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left[\frac{16\sqrt{2}L}{\lambda n}\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{\lambda}}+\frac{8\alpha}{\lambda}\right]+\alpha,

where in the last line we plugged in estimates for
\lambda
and
\Delta_{\lambda}
to bound the first two terms in the sum. Then plugging in
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}
and using the estimates and assumptions completes the proof of the first statement of the theorem. The second statement is easy to verify by plugging in the prescribed
\alpha.

Appendix D
Proofs for
section

4
and Additional Results for Non-Strongly-Convex

D.1
Proofs of Results in
section

4.1

D.1.1
Proof of
lemma

4.2

Let
x\in\mathcal{X},|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}[f(\mathcal{A^{\prime}}(X),x)-f(\mathcal{A^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}),x)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}[f(\Pi_{B(0,R)}(\widehat{w}(X)+z),x)-f(\Pi_{B(0,R)}(\widehat{w}(X^{\prime})+z),x)]

\displaystyle\leq L\|\widehat{w}(X)-\widehat{w}(X^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq L\Delta_{\widehat{F}}\leq\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu n},

using contractivity of the projection map and
proposition

2.2
. Clearly, the first inequality above also holds without projection, so that the same uniform stability bound holds for
\mathcal{A}
as well.

D.1.2
Proof of
proposition

4.1

Write

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}[F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})]=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}[F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),\mathcal{D})-\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{a}}}+\\
\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}[\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}(X),X)-\widehat{F}(\widehat{w}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{b}}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}\widehat{F}(\widehat{w}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})}_{\textcircled{\small{c}}}.\\

Now, \small{a}⃝
\leq\alpha=\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu n}
by
lemma

4.1
and
lemma

4.2
.
If
~\delta=0,
then \small{b}⃝
\leq\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right);

and if
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),
then \small{b}⃝
\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}L^{2}\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\mu\varepsilon n}
by
corollary

2.1
.
Finally, since

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}[\min_{w\in B(0,R)}\widehat{F}(w,X)]\leq\min_{w\in B(0,R)}\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}[\widehat{F}(w,X)]=F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D}),

we have that \small{c}⃝
\leq 0.
Summing these inequalities up completes the proof.

D.1.3
Proof of
corollary

4.1

Decompose

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}[F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),\mathcal{D})-\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{a}}}+\\
\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}[\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-\widehat{F}(\widehat{w}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{b}}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}}\widehat{F}(\widehat{w}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})}_{\textcircled{\small{c}}},\\

as before.
Observe that
\Delta_{T}:=\sup_{|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2}\|w_{T}(X)-w_{T}(X^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq\Delta_{\widehat{F}}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}\leq 2(\frac{L}{\mu n}+\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}),
and
\mathcal{A}
is
L\Delta_{T}
-uniformly stable with respect to
f
. Hence
\textcircled{\small{a}}\leq 2(\frac{L^{2}}{\mu n}+L\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\mu}}),
by
lemma

4.1
. Plugging in the choices of
\alpha
given in the Corollary shows that
\textcircled{\small{a}}\leq 5\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}.

Next, by
corollary

3.1
,

\textcircled{\small{b}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}[\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-\widehat{F}(\widehat{w}(X),X)]\leq 9\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}

if
\delta=0
and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{A}}[\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-\widehat{F}(\widehat{w}(X),X)]\leq 6\frac{L^{2}}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}
if
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
with the given choices of
\alpha
and
T.
Finally,
\textcircled{\small{c}}\leq 0,
as in the proof of
proposition

4.1
. Putting these estimates together completes the proof.

D.2
Population Loss Bounds for Convex, Lipschitz functions

For convex functions, we use a regularization scheme, as described in
section

2.4
and
section

C.2
, and output

w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X)=\Pi_{B(0,R)}(\widehat{w}_{\lambda}(X)+z_{\lambda}),

where
\widehat{w}_{\lambda}(X)=\argmin_{w\in B(0,R)}\widehat{F_{\lambda}}(w,X)
(c.f.
eq.

6
).
For this mechanism, we have the following expected excess population loss bounds:

Proposition D.1
.

Let
f(\cdot,x)
be
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
and convex for all
x\in\mathcal{X},
let
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Let
X
be a data set of size
n
drawn i.i.d. according to
\mathcal{D}.
Then with the choices of
\lambda>0
below, running the conceptual regularized output perturbation algorithm (
eq.

6
) on
\widehat{F}(w,X)
achieves the following expected excess population loss bounds:

a) Assume
\delta=0
and
\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1
. Set
\lambda=\left(\frac{L}{R}\right)\left(\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

Then,
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq LR\left(127\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}\right).

b) Assume
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Set
\lambda=\left(\frac{L}{R}\right)\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

Then,
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq LR\left(19\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{n}}\right).

When SGD (
algorithm

2
) is used as the non-private input to the Black Box
algorithm

7
, we achieve the above loss bounds with the following runtimes:

Corollary D.1
.

Let
f(\cdot,x)
be
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
and convex for all
x\in\mathcal{X},
let
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Let
X
be a data set of size
n
drawn i.i.d. according to
\mathcal{D}.
Run
\mathcal{A}=

algorithm

7
on
\widehat{F_{\lambda}}(w,X)
with
\mathcal{M}
as the stochastic subgradient method (
algorithm

2
) with step sizes
\eta_{t}=\frac{2}{\lambda(t+1)}
and
\lambda,~T,\alpha
as prescribed below.

a) Suppose
\delta=0,~\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\leq 1.
Set
\lambda=\frac{L}{R}\left(\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

~\alpha=\frac{LR\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{3/2}}{(1+\frac{d}{\varepsilon})^{2}},
and
T=2n^{2}\max\left\{1,(\frac{\varepsilon}{d})^{2}\right\}.
Then

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq 32LR\left(\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)

in runtime
O\left(dn^{2}\max\left\{1,(\frac{\varepsilon}{d})^{2}\right\}\right).

b) Suppose
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}).
Set
\lambda=\left(\frac{L}{R}\right)\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right),~\alpha=LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{3/2},~
and
~T=2\left(\frac{n^{2}\varepsilon}{d}\right).

Then

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq 32LR\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)

in runtime
O(n^{2}\varepsilon).

For
\delta=0,
the only competing expected excess population loss bound for this class that we are aware of is
\widetilde{O}\left(LR\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right),
obtained by the exponential mechanism in
[
BST14
]
. This bound is the same as ours. However, its worst-case runtime
\widetilde{O}(R^{2}d^{9}n^{3}\max\{d,\varepsilon nR\}),
is generally much larger than ours.
For
\delta>0,
on the other hand, there are several efficient algorithms that nearly attain the optimal (by
[
BFTGT19
]
) private expected excess population loss bound of
O\left(LR\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}\right)\right)

[
BFTGT19
,
FKT20
,
AMU20
]
. The fastest of these is the private FTRL method of
[
AMU20
]
, which has runtime
O(nd)
(and is suboptimal in excess loss by a
\log(n)
factor). However, the method does not guarantee
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differential privacy for arbitrary
\varepsilon
. In fact,
\varepsilon
must be bounded by
\sqrt{\log(\frac{1}{\delta})}
in order for their results to be valid
8
8

8

Their results are stated in terms of
(\alpha,\gamma)
-Renyi-privacy for
\alpha>1
, but they argue on p.9, using
[
FKT20
, Lemma 2.6]
, that their Algorithm 1 is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private for
\varepsilon=\frac{L\sqrt{\log(\frac{1}{\delta})}}{\sigma\sqrt{n}},
where they put
\sigma=\frac{2\alpha L}{\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\gamma}}.
Some algebra reveals that
\varepsilon=\sqrt{\log(\frac{1}{\delta})}
is necessary for their risk and privacy guarantees to both hold.
.

Smooth, convex, Lipschitz functions.

If we assume additionally that
f
is
\beta
-smooth, then the conceptual regularized output perturbation algorithm results in the following expected excess population loss guarantees:

Proposition D.2
.

Let
f(\cdot,x)
be Let
f(\cdot,x)
be
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
, convex, and
\beta
-smooth for all
x\in\mathcal{X},
let
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}).
Let
X
be a data set of size
n
drawn i.i.d. according to
\mathcal{D}.
Then with the choices of
\lambda>0
below, running the conceptual regularized output perturbation algorithm (
eq.

7
) on
\widehat{F}(w,X)
achieves the following expected excess population loss bounds:

a) If
\delta=0
and
\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{L}{R\beta},
then setting
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
implies

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{11}{\sqrt{n}}+83\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right).

b) If
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
and
\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{L}{R\beta},
then
setting
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
implies

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{11}{\sqrt{n}}+43\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right).

Next, we show that we can obtain these population loss bounds with a practical, (nearly) linear-time algorithm.

Corollary D.2
.

Let
f(\cdot,x)
be Let
f(\cdot,x)
be
L
-Lipschitz on
B(0,R)
, convex, and
\beta
-smooth for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
and let
X
be a data set of size
n
drawn i.i.d. according to
\mathcal{D}.
Run
\mathcal{A}=

algorithm

7
on
\widehat{F_{\lambda}}(w,X)
with
\mathcal{M}
as
algorithm

3
(Katyusha) with
\lambda,~\alpha,~T
as prescribed below.

1. Suppose
\delta=0
and
\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{LR}{\beta}.
Set
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

~\alpha=\frac{L^{4/3}R^{2/3}}{\beta^{1/3}},
and
~T=O\left(n+n^{5/6}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{d}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}\log\left(\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}n^{4/3}\right)\right).

Then,

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq 128\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right)

in runtime
O\left(nd+n^{5/6}d^{2/3}\varepsilon^{1/3}\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}\right).

2. Suppose
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{LR}{\beta}.

Set
\lambda=\left(\frac{\beta L^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{1/3}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right),\alpha=\frac{L^{4/3}R^{2/3}}{\beta^{1/3}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon n}{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}\right)^{2/3}\frac{1}{n^{2}},
and

~T=O\left(n+n^{5/6}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{d}(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon})}\right)^{1/3}\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}\log\left(\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}n^{2}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right)\right).

Then,

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq 128\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}\right)

in runtime
O\left(nd+n^{5/6}d^{5/6}\varepsilon^{1/6}\left(\frac{\beta R}{L}\right)^{1/3}\right).

Remark D.1
.

Since the smooth, convex, Lipschitz loss function class is a subset of the convex, Lipschitz loss function class, we obtain by combining
proposition

D.1
with
corollary

D.2
the following upper bounds on the expected excess population loss of smooth, strongly convex, Lipschitz functions:

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\lesssim\min\left\{LR,LR\left(\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right),\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right\}
for
\delta=0
; and
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}^{\delta}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\lesssim\min\left\{LR,LR\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right),\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{2/3}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right\}
for
\delta>0.
The second terms in each of the above minima are the non-smooth expected excess population losses from
proposition

D.1
. For smooth convex loss functions, by utilizing Katyusha, these expected excess population losses can be realized faster than the runtime promised in
corollary

D.1
. To derive these enhanced upper bounds on the runtimes for obtaining the non-smooth convex expected excess population loss bounds, recall the procedure described in
remark

C.1
: use the regularization term
\lambda
(and the corresponding
\alpha
) prescribed in
theorem

C.1
. Plugging this into the runtime expression for Katyusha gives the following runtime for achieving excess empirical risk
O(LR\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}})
for
\delta=0
:
\widetilde{O}(d(n+\sqrt{n\left(\frac{\beta+\lambda}{\lambda}\right)}))=\widetilde{O}\left(nd+n^{3/4}d^{3/4}\varepsilon^{1/4}\sqrt{\frac{\beta R}{L}}\right).
By the uniform stability argument used in this section, this excess empirical loss bound implies expected excess population loss of
O\left(LR\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}}\right)\right)
. This explains where the “
\max\{...\}
” term in the last row (corresponding to
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}
) of
table

3
comes from. Likewise, for
\delta>0,
the runtime for obtaining excess empirical risk
O\left(LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}\right)
, which yields the expected excess population loss
O\left(LR\left(\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)
, by Katyusha is
\widetilde{O}\left(nd+n^{3/4}d^{7/8}\varepsilon^{1/8}\sqrt{\frac{\beta R}{L}}\right).

Recall that the optimal non-private population loss for convex Lipschitz is
O(\frac{LR}{\sqrt{n}}).

Hence, if we assume
\beta R=L
, so that
\beta^{1/3}L^{2/3}R^{4/3}=LR
for simplicity, then when
\delta=0,
we get privacy for free if
\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}\lesssim\sqrt{n}.
For
\delta>0,
under the same simplifying assumptions on
\beta,L,R
, we get privacy for free if
\frac{dc_{\delta}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}.
For general
\beta,L,
and
R,
the “privacy for free” parameter ranges may be narrower than these by a factor of
\left(\frac{L}{\beta R}\right)^{1/3}
.

For
\delta=0
, there are no other private expected population loss bounds we are aware of besides the one of
[
BST14
]
mentioned above, which, to the best of our knowledge, does not improve under additional smoothness assumption and is therefore inferior to our bounds in both excess loss and runtime. The gradient descent-based output perturbation method of
[
ZZMW17
]
achieves with probability
~1-\gamma
, excess population loss of
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{RL^{1/2}(\beta d)^{1/4}}{(n\varepsilon\gamma)^{1/2}}\right),
but they do not provide a guarantee for
expected
excess population loss. For
\delta>0,

[
BFTGT19
]
,
[
FKT20
]
, and
[
AMU20
]
all nearly achieve the optimal (by
[
BFTGT19
]
) expected excess population loss bound
O\left(LR\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon n}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)
for this function class efficiently. In fact,
[
FKT20
]
achieves the bound up to a
\log(\frac{1}{\delta})
factor in runtime
O(nd)
, provided
\frac{\beta R}{L}\lesssim\min\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)n}}{\sqrt{d}}\right\}
and
\varepsilon\leq 2\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right).
The first condition is not very restrictive; indeed, essentially every method (including ours) requires some such restriction to guarantee non-trivial excess risk for
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,L,R}.
The condition on
\varepsilon,
however does restrict the range of
n,d
in which strong risk bounds can be realized privately. Our method has no such restriction.

The last noteworthy result from recent DP SCO literature is that conceptual objective perturbation can attain obtain the optimal
(\varepsilon,\delta)
private population excess loss in the convex smooth case under strict assumptions such as the loss function
f
having rank-one hessian and
\beta\leq\frac{2\varepsilon L}{R}\sqrt{2n+4d\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}

[
BFTGT19
]
. By contrast, here we have shown that output perturbation, which does not require any of the above assumptions and can be implemented quite quickly and easily, nearly attains the optimal rate and also applies (and performs quite well) for
\delta=0
.

Proof of
proposition

4.2

The proof is nearly identical to the proof of

4.1
, except that we use the excess empirical risk bounds for
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,\mu,L,R}^{ERM}
(
corollary

2.2
) to bound the \small{b}⃝ term instead of
corollary

2.1
.

D.2.2
Proof of
corollary

4.2

The proof follows exactly as the proof of
corollary

4.1
, but using
corollary

3.2
instead of
corollary

3.1
to bound \small{b}⃝.

D.3
Proofs of Results in
section

D.2

D.3.1
Proof of
proposition

D.1

As in the proof of
proposition

4.1
, write

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}[F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{a}}}+\\
\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}[\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-\widehat{F}(\widehat{w}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{b}}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}\widehat{F}(\widehat{w}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})}_{\textcircled{\small{c}}}.\\

Now decompose \small{a}⃝ into three parts:

\displaystyle\textcircled{\small{a}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-F_{\lambda}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{i}}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[F_{\lambda}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-\widehat{F_{\lambda}}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{ii}}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\widehat{F_{\lambda}}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{iii}}},

where
F_{\lambda}(w,\mathcal{D}):=F(w,\mathcal{D})+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|w\|_{2}^{2}.

Then \small{i}⃝ + \small{iii}⃝ = 0, whereas \small{ii}⃝
\leq\frac{2(L+\lambda R)^{2}}{\lambda n}
by
lemma

4.1
and
lemma

4.2
. Next, decompose \small{b}⃝ into three parts as in the proof of
proposition

2.7
:

\displaystyle\textcircled{\small{b}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-\widehat{F_{\lambda}}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{1}}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\widehat{F_{\lambda}}(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),X)-\widehat{F_{\lambda}}(\widehat{w_{\lambda}}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{{\small{2}}}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\widehat{F_{\lambda}}(\widehat{w_{\lambda}})(X),X)-\widehat{F}(\widehat{w}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{{\small{3}}}}.

Then
\textcircled{{\small{1}}}\leq 0.
Also,

\textcircled{{\small{2}}}\leq(L+\lambda R)\mathbb{E}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}.

By the definition of
z_{\lambda}
(
section

2.4
),
lemma

B.3
, and
proposition

2.2
, we have
\mathbb{E}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}(L+\lambda R)}{\lambda}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)
if
\delta=0;
if
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),
then
\mathbb{E}\|z_{\lambda}\|_{2}\leq\frac{2(L+\lambda R)}{\lambda}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right).

The third term
\textcircled{\small{3}}\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}.
Lastly,
\textcircled{\small{c}}\leq 0
by the same argument used in
proposition

4.1
. Hence if
~\delta=0,
then

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq 2\sqrt{2}\left[\frac{(L+\lambda R)^{2}}{\lambda}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)\right]+\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2},

and if
~\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),
then

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})\leq 2\left[\frac{(L+\lambda R)^{2}}{\lambda}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)\right]+\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{2}.

For
\delta=0,
plugging in
\lambda=\left(\frac{L}{R}\right)\left(\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
gives the first result.

For
\delta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right),
instead choose
\lambda=\left(\frac{L}{R}\right)\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}\left(c_{\delta}+\sqrt{c_{\delta}^{2}+\varepsilon}\right)}{\varepsilon n}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
to get the last result.

D.3.2
Proof of
corollary

D.1

Decompose

\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}F(w_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}},\lambda}(X),\mathcal{D})-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}[F(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),\mathcal{D})-\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{a}}}+\\
\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}[\widehat{F}(w_{\mathcal{A}}(X),X)-\widehat{F}(\widehat{w}(X),X)]}_{\textcircled{\small{b}}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D}^{n},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}_{\lambda}}\widehat{F}(\widehat{w}(X),X)-F(w^{*}(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{D})}_{\textcircled{\small{c}}},

as before. First,
\mathcal{A}
is
L\Delta_{T}
-uniformly stable with respect to
f
(see proof of
corollary

4.1
), and
\Delta_{T}=\sup_{|X\Delta X^{\prime}|\leq 2}\|w_{T}(X)-w_{T}(X^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq\Delta_{\lambda}+2\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}}\leq 2\left[\frac{L+\lambda R}{\lambda n}+\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}}\right],
using
corollary

C.1
and strong convexity of
F_{\lambda}
. Hence
\textcircled{\small{a}}\leq 2L[\frac{L+\lambda R}{\lambda n}+\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda}}]
by
